We propose Generalized Proximal Policy Optimization (GPPO), a principled and unified framework for understanding and designing LLM RL algorithms through the lens of divergence. We establish a generalized $f$-divergence trust-region theory with a monotonic performance improvement guarantee, and show that different $f$-divergences induce distinct geometries and policy update dynamics. We further introduce novel estimator topologies that provide more faithful divergence estimates than the conventional single-sample Monte Carlo estimation. Under this framework, PPO and recent variants such as DAPO, DPPO, and RIPO emerge as special cases, establishing a principled two-dimensional design space of {Divergence $\times$ Estimator}. Extensive experiments on eight competition-level math and coding benchmarks show that both dimensions are critical. The best-performing GPPO instantiation surpasses standard GRPO by up to 50% and 14% on math and coding, delivering SOTA performance.

</details>


### [176] [Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13020)
*Zhicheng Cai,Weinan Dai,Yuyan Zhou,Wei-Ying Ma,Ya-Qin Zhang,Hao Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability.

</details>


### [177] [Low-Rank Kolmogorov-Arnold Generator Flows for Interpretable Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13028)
*Minjong Cheon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Neural operators that learn physical laws from data should generalize beyond their training distribution and expose the learned law in closed form. The Kolmogorov-Arnold neural operator achieves both for position-dependent dynamics by learning a dense pseudo-differential symbol over all positions and frequencies, at a cost that grows with the square of the resolution and with closed forms that must be read out by hand. This dense symbol is unnecessary: a finite-order variable-coefficient differential operator has a symbol whose separation rank is bounded by the number of its coefficient functions, independently of the resolution, and the symbols that this network learns on its own benchmarks have rank two. We introduce the Kolmogorov-Arnold flow (KAFlow), which learns the generator of a physical operator as a low-rank symbol built from univariate Kolmogorov-Arnold functions, fixes the gauge of these functions so that every term has a unique place, integrates the dynamics with exact exponential integrators, and distills its closed form automatically. On the synthetic benchmarks of the Kolmogorov-Arnold neural operator, KAFlow lowers the out-of-distribution loss by 3.4 to 11 times and recovers every coefficient to within 10^-4 without symbolic templates, reaching the precision floor of the data. On long-horizon quantum dynamics, it lowers the state infidelity by up to 6 orders of magnitude, and by 30 times when trained only on position and momentum measurements. KAFlow also learns a variable-order fractional operator 15 times more accurately than existing operator networks and discovers nonlocal, fractional and non-polynomial dispersion relations on which sparse regression fails.

</details>


### [178] [Target-Constrained Pooling for Trajectory Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13034)
*Ning Wang,Claire Cardie,Sainyam Galhotra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Trajectory extrapolation from a partial target trajectory is underdetermined, but completed related trajectories provide information about the unresolved continuation. We propose target-constrained pooling (TCP), which separates the finite-horizon endpoint from continuation shape and estimates that endpoint from two complementary source signals. Uniform pooling captures final response levels, while matched-increment pooling captures the remaining change after aligning source and target states. TCP selects representative sources, projects both endpoint estimates onto a target-consistent region, converts them into corrections of the free forecast, and controls their influence through strength calibration and degradation-aware weighting. Across 25,245 targets from five domains and both forecasting directions, TCP reduces mean RMSE by 35.8% to 42.1% for all ten target-only forecasters and improves all 100 forecaster, dataset, and direction comparisons. The two endpoint signals consistently outperform either alone, while representative selection remains within 0.64% to 1.02% of the full donor pool using 12.7x fewer donor inputs and 138.8x fewer matching distance computations.

</details>


### [179] [Towards Easier-to-Tune Training Algorithms: New Results from AlgoPerf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13036)
*Priya Kasimbeg,Ahmed Khaled,Aahlad Chandrabhatta,Yifan Wang,Andy Liu,Niccolò Ajroldi,David Tweedle,Xingyu,Yang,Isaac Patole,Frank Schneider,George E. Dahl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Evaluating deep learning optimizer is challenging because confounding factors such as hyperparameter tuning and optimizer-model co-design often obscure true algorithmic performance. We present results of the updated AlgoPerf V1 benchmark, in which we evaluate a new set of training algorithms on wall-clock time-to-target across nine workloads on fixed hardware. This report focuses on the results of the benchmark's self-tuning ruleset, which requires submissions to use a single configuration across all workloads. Out of the 13 submissions in PyTorch and JAX, Schedule-Free AdamW ranked first. In addition to the wall-clock scores, we also analyze the trade-offs between step efficiency and wall-clock execution, revealing that matrix-preconditioned and two-loop methods achieve good sample efficiency but require more aggressive computational optimizations to be competitive in runtime. Finally, our hyperparameter saliency and retention analyses show that the learning rate and momentum are highly sensitive across domains and algorithms, highlighting the need for methods that are more robust against hyperparameter tuning and can generalize well to new tasks.

</details>


### [180] [CB-Orchestrator: Adaptive Workflow Optimization for LLM Agents via Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13064)
*Jiahang Sun,Zhiwei Shang,Zhipiao Liu,Hongwei Yang,Guoqing Xie,Shuang Qiu,Zhongxiang Dai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in tackling complex tasks through agentic workflows. However, manually designing these workflows is labor-intensive and lacks flexibility to adapt to diverse queries. While automated workflow optimization is a promising direction, existing methods often incur expensive API costs during training-phase feedback collection. Furthermore, they fail to adaptively reuse high-quality workflows, which creates a bottleneck for both performance and cost-effectiveness. To address these limitations, we propose CB-Orchestrator, an adaptive framework that decouples workflow generation from selection. In the first stage, we construct a diverse pool of candidate workflows via evolutionary search. In the second stage, we formulate workflow selection as a Contextual Bandit problem, which enables sample-efficient learning by balancing exploration and exploitation, thereby significantly reducing the feedback required for training. During inference, the model adaptively selects the optimal workflow tailored to each query. Evaluations across five diverse benchmarks demonstrate that CB-Orchestrator consistently outperforms all baselines. Notably, on the MATH dataset, CB-Orchestrator achieves better performance than strong recent baselines while reducing training-phase API token overhead by no less than 40.55% and total end-to-end training (including workflow pool generation) token overhead by no less than 29.73%.

</details>


### [181] [Human and LLM Facilitation in Collaborative Design Meetings: A Computational Behavioral Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13082)
*Mohammed Azeez Khan,Aaron Dsouza,Ashutosh Mishra,Ummul Faiz Z. Bibi,Neha K. Nair,Amar Kumar Behera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored as facilitators of group discussion and collaborative work, but the behavioral differences between LLM-generated and human facilitation remain insufficiently characterized. We compare human Project Manager facilitation turns from scenario-based collaborative design meetings with Llama 3.1 8B Instruct responses across 199 paired contexts from the AMI Meeting Corpus. The analysis covers five confirmatory dimensions: contextual semantic distance, a lexical elaboration proxy, empathyand-hedge lexical density, topic divergence, and phase-vocabulary alignment. An exploratory directiveness measure failed domain validation and is not interpreted. The sampled human Project Manager turns were higher on contextual semantic distance (r = 0.644) and on empathyand-hedge lexical density (r = 0.203). The second contrast is carried mainly by hedge terms, so we read it as lexical hedging rather than psychological empathy. Llama-generated facilitation was higher on topic divergence (r = 0.703), the lexical elaboration proxy (r = 0.288; lengthconfounded), and instruction-conditioned phase-vocabulary alignment (r = 0.382). A secondary no-phase check left the Llama-higher phase contrast in place. The system prompt still requested a facilitation move, so that check is not evidence of spontaneous phase awareness. These differences should not be interpreted as a ranking of facilitation quality. The resulting behavioral profile provides a reference point for later comparisons of LLM facilitators, human facilitators, prompts, models, and collaborative settings.

</details>


### [182] [Can Failed Trajectories Reveal Where Agents Go Wrong? Agentic Failure Attribution Without Step-Level Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13105)
*Do Nguyen-Thanh,Junlin Fang,Bing Li,Zhen Fang,Sean Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Failure attribution for LLM agents aims to identify decisive steps responsible for task failure, which is critical for debugging and improving agentic systems. Existing methods rely on costly LLM prompting, step-level error annotations, or verified successful trajectories that lack failure signals for assistance. In this paper, we study failure attribution from step-unlabeled failure trajectories, where each trajectory is known to have failed but its decisive step is unknown. We propose SOAP (Spectral scOring with Attention-guided Propagation), an effective framework requiring neither step-level training labels nor a separate success-only reference set. SOAP first constructs a spectral reference space from LLM representations and assigns each step a base error score according to its projection behavior. Importantly, later steps may exhibit stronger failure signals because they inherit an earlier mistake, causing independent scorers to favor downstream consequences over the decisive error. To address this downstream contamination, SOAP derives soft step dependencies from attention patterns and propagates downstream error evidence toward its likely upstream sources. Extensive experiments show that SOAP consistently improves attribution performance over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [183] [Scaling RNA Foundation Models through Iterative Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13106)
*Pengan Chen,Liang Hong,Xinyuan Liu,Han Huang,Ziqian Lin,Jiayang Chen,Yimin Fan,Jiyue Jiang,Yu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Biological function in RNA is encoded across scales, from local sequence motifs to long-range structural dependencies. Conventional RNA foundation models couple parameter capacity and inference depth within a fixed architecture, prescribing a fixed computation graph for each inference. Here we present Eco-RNA, an 80M-parameter RNA foundation model that makes inference depth adjustable through learned iterative refinement. Its shared, input-conditioned Transformer core refines sequence representations through repeated application. The iteration count is selected after training and controls effective depth at fixed model parameters. We train across recurrent depths with masked reconstruction, state regularization, and a one-sided loss that anchors deeper reconstruction to a shallow reference. On the 70-assay RNAGym fitness benchmark, increasing refinement from one to four iterations raises aggregate absolute Spearman correlation from 0.242 to 0.286, with the gain retained through eight iterations. At four iterations, Eco-RNA surpasses the current SOTA Evo 2 40B on all three aggregate fitness metrics despite 500x fewer parameters. Its iteration configurations advance the compute-performance Pareto frontier among the evaluated models. It also outperforms the evaluated baselines in non-coding RNA family classification, RNA secondary-structure prediction, and chemical probing profile prediction. These results establish internal inference computation as an effective scaling dimension for RNA foundation models.

</details>


### [184] [Continually Post-training Small Models via Synthetic Self-distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13113)
*Woosung Koh,Hyunsoo Lee,Kyungjae Lee,Haeju Park,Dahyun Lee,Dasol Hwang,Moontae Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Continual post-training faces a dual challenge of acquisition and retention: models must learn new capabilities while preserving prior knowledge. While Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT) mitigates this using self-supervision with in-context demonstrations, it relies on strong in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. Consequently, SDFT fails on smaller models critical for low-latency, on-device use. To enable continual learning at smaller scales, we first identify the root cause of failure. We show that poor acquisition and forgetting correlate linearly with the post-trained model's distribution shift, revealing the necessity of in-distribution learning. We thus introduce Synthetic Self-Distillation (SynthSelf), imitating SDFT's in-distribution rewrite by outsourcing the task to a minimally larger, same-family aligner. Coupled with off-policy supervision and rejection sampling, this provides the high-quality supervision small models require without breaking their original distribution. Evaluations spanning 4 acquisition datasets (including a mixture) and 14 retention benchmarks show SynthSelf consistently outperforms 6 methods (including off- and on-policy distillation) across 3 small model scales. Extensive further analyses confirm SynthSelf paves the way toward effective small-model continual learning.

</details>


### [185] [WaSA: Wavelet Scattering Autoencoders for Unsupervised Offline Detection of Malicious Clients in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13186)
*Alessandro Licciardi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative model training without exposing raw data, yet remains vulnerable to malicious clients injecting poisoned datasets. Existing defenses are typically reactive (operating during training) or supervised (requiring prior knowledge of attack types) - assumptions that rarely hold in open-ended IoT deployments. We propose WaSA, a fully unsupervised, offline framework for malicious client detection. Before training, each client transmits a single, privacy-preserving spectral summary of its data to the server. A denoising autoencoder, trained solely on clean auxiliary data, scores clients based on their deviation from the learned benign manifold, identifying corruptions regardless of the attack type. WaSA requires no attack labels, generalizes to unseen anomalies, and introduces a Boltzmann sampling strategy for client selection that smoothly interpolates between hard exclusion and standard aggregation. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 under diverse attack scenarios (up to 90 % malicious clients) demonstrate consistent improvements over supervised baselines and robust aggregation methods, with negligible communication overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/alelicciardi99/wavelet_autoencoders_malicious.

</details>


### [186] [Does Learning Recurrent Networks Matter in Partially Observable RL?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13197)
*Zekang Wang,Zhe He,Gaojie Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) under partial observability typically re- lies on recurrent networks to encode temporal information, with the recurrent module trained jointly with the policy or value function. This raises a natural question: is learning the recurrent network actually necessary? In this paper, we investigate whether a frozen recurrent network can provide sufficient memory for solving par- tially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs). Motivated by the theoretical foundations of Reservoir Computing (RC) and Generalized Reservoir Computing (GRC), we study a simple archi- tecture that combines a fixed recurrent network with a trainable nonlinear output module. We evaluate this approach across tasks with varying memory demands, observation modalities, and action spaces. Surprisingly, frozen recurrent networks support recall over hundreds of steps and achieve competitive control performance across diverse POMDPs, while using less than half the number of trainable parameters required by their fully trainable counterparts. These results suggest that learning the recurrent memory module is not always necessary for solving POMDPs. Frozen recurrent networks therefore provide a simple, parameter-efficient, and sur- prisingly strong baseline for partially observable RL. Our source code is available at https://github.com/Aphlios/FRN-POMDP

</details>


### [187] [S$^3$R: Fast and Faithful RL Rollouts via Self-Sparse Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13202)
*Yuhang He,Hongyu Ge,Zihan Lin,Siyi Liu,Yongqi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Fast rollout generation for long-context reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) requires reducing KV-cache traffic without changing the response groups used for policy optimization. In our profiled RLVR workloads, attention accounts for 72--76\% of autoregressive decoding kernel time. Sparse attention reduces these reads, but sampling directly from a sparse policy changes the response groups used for learning. For group-relative optimization, we show that even exact response-wise reweighting does not generally recover the expected dense-policy update because the advantage of each response depends on the rewards of other group members. We propose Self-Sparse Speculative Rollouts (S$^3$R). It uses sparse attention to propose tokens, then generates responses through full-attention verification and exact speculative sampling. Drafting and verification share the same policy parameter, eliminating the need to train or update a separate drafter. The resulting response groups follow the dense-policy distribution. Under S$^3$R, we characterize speedup conditions and the costs of drafting and verification. We found that dense verification still requires the complete historical KV cache. A KV-aware scheduler therefore limits KV-cache repeated offloading and restoration while maintaining rollout parallelism. % OR thrashing In a matched ablation with an aggressive sparse drafting budget, S$^3$R maintains stable training while direct sparse sampling with training-side correction becomes unstable. On long-context mathematical reasoning, S$^3$R achieves $1.39\times~1.35\times$ the end-to-end training throughput of autoregressive rollouts on Qwen3 model, with comparable task performance.

</details>


### [188] [Rethinking Relative Policy Differences Transfer of On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13205)
*Rong Wu,Daocheng Fu,Yu Yang,Xuemeng Yang,Siqi Li,Shu Zou,Yao Zhu,Botian Shi,Bo Zhang,Cong Zhang,Pinlong Cai,Jianbiao Mei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a target policy on its own rollouts using token-level supervision from a stronger teacher, but typically relies on the teacher being substantially more capable than the target. Recent work relaxes this requirement by transferring the relative policy difference between an expert and a reference policy rather than the expert's absolute distribution. Yet it remains unclear which differences provide useful supervision and which targets can benefit from them. We develop a unified view in which the log-probability ratio between a proxy expert and a proxy reference defines an implicit reward for KL-regularized policy optimization. This formulation induces an on-policy update for the target, recovers conventional OPD as a special case, and enables any-to-any transfer across models that differ in scale, training stage or generation. Our systematic experiments yield three findings. First, a stronger expert is not necessarily a better source of supervision: differences induced by task-specific training, including reinforcement learning and supervised fine-tuning, transfer consistently, whereas those arising from model scale alone transfer weakly or not at all. Second, the expert and reference serve complementary roles: the expert provides the capability to be transferred, while the reference filters out shared behavior and isolates the relevant residual. Third, even a useful difference does not transfer to every target: success requires the induced update to move the target toward the expert and generalize beyond sampled rollouts. Raw policy distance does not predict transferability, whereas an appropriate reference can rescue otherwise unsuccessful transfer. These results characterize when relative policy differences provide effective supervision for OPD.

</details>


### [189] [How Much Computation Does Every Forecast Need? Structured Block Skipping in Two Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13208)
*Max Bourgeat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Time-series foundation models use the same network depth for every input, although forecasting windows differ in structure and difficulty. We test whether whole Transformer blocks can be removed at inference time in two pretrained models: Chronos-Bolt and TimesFM 2.5. Our bypasses avoid the original block computation and preserve the dense model exactly when all blocks are enabled. Masks are selected only on calibration windows and evaluated on 1,088 held-out windows from ETTh1, Traffic, Weather, and Exchange. At moderate sparsity, Chronos-Bolt with 7/11 candidate blocks and TimesFM with 16/20 blocks show little change in mean relative forecasting quality across datasets while reducing median A100 batch-1 latency by 11.2% and 13.4%. More aggressive TimesFM skipping accelerates inference further but substantially harms Traffic and Weather. Across both models, different equal-budget masks win on different windows, and a retrospective oracle significantly improves over the best static mask on every dataset. However, dataset-specific masks transfer inconsistently, and Chronos representations do not support reliable unseen-domain routing. These results establish cross-architecture structured redundancy and input-dependent opportunity, but not a deployable dynamic inference method.

</details>


### [190] [Monarch-MoE: Structured Experts for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13215)
*Xi Zhang,Boyu Diao,Zesen Chen,Ruiqi Liu,Hangda Liu,Yongjun Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Learning new tasks in multimodal continual instruction tuning can cause catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired knowledge. Although multi-expert LoRA distributes learning across experts, updates to a dense low-rank expert can still affect many output features. We introduce Monarch-MoE, which replaces the dense factors in LoRA experts with block-diagonal factors connected by a fixed permutation. This structure provides blockwise gradient isolation: individual block-pair updates affect only one output block, while connections between all input and output blocks are maintained. To make these compact experts practical on memory-limited hardware, we combine batched computation, fused forward operators, and an optimized backward pass. Across three continual-learning methods evaluated on MLLM-DCL and UCIT with LLaVA-1.5-7B, Monarch-MoE improves first-task retention while reducing adapter parameters by approximately 75% and 50%, respectively. In the main comparison, first-task forgetting is as low as 0.52 percentage points on MLLM-DCL and 0.10 on UCIT, with method-dependent trade-offs in final performance. Our implementation supports fine-tuning and inference on a single 24 GB RTX 4090, with lower measured memory use and competitive runtime in the evaluated settings. Together, these algorithmic and systems improvements make structured experts a practical option for continual adaptation on memory-limited hardware.

</details>


### [191] [Tensor-based Fusion of Multi-modal Data for Predicting Cancer Risk Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13221)
*Ali Anaissi,Yuanzhe Jia,Qishun Peng,Weidong Huang,Ali Braytee,Junaid Akram*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Cancer diagnosis and treatment increasingly rely on multi-modal diagnostic technologies that generate high-dimensional datasets. Although these datasets are rich in pathological information, they pose considerable challenges in interpretation, dimensionality reduction, and clinical applicability. Conventional machine learning approaches often fail to extract meaningful cross-modal features, thereby limiting predictive accuracy in patient risk stratification. To address these limitations, this paper proposes an unsupervised machine learning framework that integrates feature extraction, tensor decomposition, and clustering to improve cancer risk prediction. The framework employs tensor decomposition to fuse heterogeneous genomic and clinical data, enabling the extraction of latent, biologically meaningful features that outperform unimodal feature sets. Multiple feature extraction strategies are investigated, and the resulting features are subsequently clustered using a range of clustering algorithms. Comparative evaluations based on internal and external metrics are then used to identify the most effective configurations. The framework is validated on LinkedOmics data comprising 11,158 patients across 32 cancer types. For patient risk stratification, clinically relevant attributes such as tumor purity, pathological stage, and survival outcomes serve as ground-truth indicators. Experimental results demonstrate the superiority of tSVD+GMM, which consistently outperforms other configurations across clinical benchmarks.

</details>


### [192] [ReSFlow: Rectifying Categorical Flows on Statistical Manifolds for One-Step Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13225)
*Xiaohuan Jia,Renzhe Xu,Xiao Wang,Jiayun Wu,Shaohua Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Generative modeling for discrete data has been significantly advanced by learning continuous vector fields on the statistical manifolds to transport simple priors to target discrete distributions. However, because these continuous-time flows are not constrained to follow geodesics on the statistical manifold, thereby requiring multi-step numerical integration for simulation and leading to substantial computational costs during inference. In this work, drawing inspiration from the "straight and fast" properties of Rectified Flow in continuous Euclidean space, we propose the Rectified Statistical Flow (ReSFlow) model, which enables one-step categorical generation on statistical manifolds by explicitly learning geodesic trajectories that adhere to the intrinsic Riemannian geometry. By rectifying the flow on the statistical manifold, we theoretically guarantee that trajectories between prior and target distributions approximate geodesics. This rectification significantly reduces the transport cost by aligning the learned trajectories directly along geodesics on the statistical manifold, allowing the model to map pure noise to target discrete data in a single step. Through extensive experiments on diverse real-world generative tasks, ranging from image and text to biological domains, we demonstrate ReSFlow's superior one-step generation quality, which truly embodies geodesically optimal and rapid categorical generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/xiaohuanjia/ReSFlow.

</details>


### [193] [Learning Schwarzschild Geodesic Motion with Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13234)
*Sukru Caglar,Sena Kalabalik,Abdullah Umut Hamzaogullari,Arkadas Ozakin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) learn dynamics from trajectory data by parameterizing a scalar Lagrangian and deriving accelerations through the Euler--Lagrange equation, providing a variational inductive bias that improves physical consistency over purely data-driven models. LNNs have recently been extended to general relativistic settings, leaving Schwarzschild geodesics as an open challenge. Training on complex relativistic systems is hindered by the diversity of the dynamics, as well as numerical instabilities and the difficulty of adequately sampling physically relevant, diverse orbital regimes. We introduce two coordinated improvements: a decoupled position--velocity normalization scheme that independently rescales each coordinate and its time derivatives, reweighting acceleration targets to balance contributions across all channels; and an energy-constrained log-uniformly sampled dataset generation strategy informed by the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO) structure. With these improvements, our model accurately reproduces perihelion precession and relativistic time dilation, conserves energy and angular momentum to within a few percent even after completing multiple orbits around the central mass and predicts orbital trajectories across eight orbital scenarios in close, medium, and far-field regimes. These results establish that LNNs, augmented with physically motivated normalization and sampling, can reliably learn geodesic dynamics in curved spacetime. Our work also represents the first successful training of LNNs on Schwarzschild geodesics and offers a promising direction for data-driven modeling of complex relativistic systems.

</details>


### [194] [State-OPD: State-Grounded On-Policy Distillation for Multi-Turn Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13238)
*Zhentao Zhang,Yanzhao Zheng,Yanlin Liu,Yuanqiang Yu,Tianze Xu,Chao Ma,Jihuai Zhu,Lelun Wu,Baohua Dong,Hangcheng Zhu,Ruohui Huang,Gang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: On-Policy Distillation (OPD) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for transferring reasoning capabilities from frontier models to smaller models, while extending its success to multi-turn agentic environments remains challenging. We study agentic OPD through interaction states, distinguishing the objective task and executed action-observation record from the internal semantic trajectory of student reasoning. Through this lens, we identify two roles of student reasoning in multi-turn OPD: prior reasoning distorts teacher supervision, while current reasoning shapes the action alternatives sampled at the same state. Specifically, at matched interaction states, removing prior student reasoning improves teacher continuation success and changes the teacher's token-level targets, with larger continuation gains at later turns. Meanwhile, resampling complete reasoning-action responses yields greater action diversity at the same state than resampling actions under a fixed reasoning trace. We introduce State-Grounded On-Policy Distillation, a framework that grounds teacher supervision in interaction states and reallocates the sampling budget to generate multiple complete reasoning-action responses at states with high response entropy, while retaining full-history student generation. Comprehensive evaluations across ALFWorld, ScienceWorld and WebShop demonstrate that State-OPD achieves the highest macro-average success rate among the evaluated methods for both Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B students. Notably, for Qwen3-4B, State-OPD raises macro-average success from 26.07\% with the strongest baseline to 31.74\% and surpasses its Qwen3-32B teacher on ALFWorld Seen. Further analyses reveal that State-OPD achieves higher task success with fewer supervised turn responses and substantially reduces persistent failed-action repetition; qualitative traces illustrate decision revision based on interaction feedback.

</details>


### [195] [Tabular Foundation Models Are Effectively Shallow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13253)
*Irene Cannistraci,Julia E. Vogt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: In-context-learning Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) are uniquely suited to scenarios with limited resources or privacy constraints, as they solve new tasks without additional training. However, their architecture consists of multiple transformer blocks, resulting in high computational and memory footprints. Meanwhile, traditional alternatives like Gradient-Boosted Decision Trees (GBDTs) remain highly competitive and run efficiently on CPUs, suggesting that tabular tasks may not inherently require deep architectures. This contrast raises a fundamental question: can TFMs be simplified by removing architectural redundancies without sacrificing performance? To answer this, we introduce a closed-form, label-free affine translator that replaces a contiguous window of up to 96% of the blocks, leaving the rest unchanged. Evaluating eight frontier TFMs on 171 classification datasets, we reveal that tabular transformers are vastly overparameterized: replacing half of a model's blocks costs at most $0.002$ median AUROC across all architectures, with the half-depth TabFM still outperforming every other full-depth TFMs and tuned GBDTs. Furthermore, different model families are overparameterized in different ways: TabPFN-3 relies on its encoders, maintaining performance regardless of which single block is retained. Other single-axis models spread computation thinly, needing only their first block, while dual-axis models concentrate it in one critical block. Ultimately, removing depth saves up to 1.5B parameters, speeds up GPU inference by up to $6\times$ at a median cost of $0.03$ AUROC, while a single-block TabPFN-3 serves cached queries $2.4\times$ faster using $6\times$ less memory at negligible cost.

</details>


### [196] [CAST: Credit Alignment for Cross-Tokenizer On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13257)
*Jingru Yang,Jinhang Wu,Minjing Shi,Shengfeng He,Xuyan Li,Man Tan,Biye Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Cross-tokenizer on-policy distillation (OPD) requires aligning teacher and student sequences before their scores can be compared. Decoded-text alignment provides such correspondence at the chunk level, but leaves a distinct problem unresolved: credit alignment. A chunk-level signal specifies how much a span should change, but not how that signal should be distributed across student tokens, while tokens without teacher correspondence remain unconstrained. We propose CAST (Chunk-aware credit Assignment with Selective Token Anchoring), a shift-or-stay framework that addresses both issues. For aligned chunks, CAST redistributes each chunk's advantage across student tokens using predictive uncertainty and local teacher-signal smoothness, emphasizing learning opportunities supported by locally consistent teacher feedback while preserving the chunk's total advantage. The resulting weights exactly solve a KL-regularized credit-allocation problem. For unaligned positions, CAST selectively anchors the student to its initial policy through a KL penalty, limiting drift where teacher supervision is unavailable. Because credit assignment is separated from chunk construction, CAST also applies to punctuation-defined or fixed-length chunks under shared tokenization. Across six mathematics, science, and code benchmarks, CAST improves the average over the strongest evaluated baseline by $2.45$ and $1.32$ points for cross-tokenizer transfer from Qwen3-8B to Mistral-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, respectively, and by $2.10$ points for shared-tokenizer transfer to Qwen3-1.7B.

</details>


### [197] [Neural Surrogate Modeling of Ultrasonic Wave Propagation in Heterogeneous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13258)
*Burla Nur Korkmaz,Petro Moilanen,Ari Salmi,Harsha Vardhan Tetali,Arto Klami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Neural surrogates provide computationally efficient auto-regressive modeling of wave propagation, enabling e.g. fast inverse solutions. Despite recent progress, the current surrogates are not yet sufficient for the needs of ultrasonic sensing, characterized by high frequency and heterogeneous propagation media. We show how methods building on Fourier Neural Operators (FNO) fail to capture essential signal features, such as reflections, whereas U-Net -based methods learn the wave fields well but lack the ability to transfer information across different spatial scales. We then propose a new surrogate that captures the best of the two worlds, by integrating FNOs and local-integral resampling into a U-Net architecture. We show it can be trained on limited data, and that the predictions are robust to input noise and work across spatial scales.

</details>


### [198] [Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Metric Learning for Cross-Head Attention in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13261)
*Zhenke Duan,Xin Li,Jiqun Pan,Dong Xiaofei,Hanwen Ning,Xinyuan Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: In this paper, we propose Data-Adaptive Mahalanobis Cross-Head Attention (DAMCHA), a simple and effective attention mechanism that generalizes Multi-Head Attention (MHA) through the lens of metric learning. Standard MHA implicitly learns a static, head-wise Euclidean similarity corresponding to the diagonal blocks of a unified cross-head metric matrix, discarding all off-diagonal interactions across heads. DAMCHA addresses this limitation via two key innovations. First, it learns the full cross-head metric matrix, inducing a Mahalanobis-like similarity that explicitly captures inter-head interactions beyond the restricted block-diagonal structure of MHA. Second, it parameterizes the metric matrix as an input-dependent function, enabling the attention geometry to dynamically adapt to the intrinsic manifold structure of the data rather than remaining fixed after training. We further introduce principled regularization strategies, most notably stack-wise parameter sharing, to ensure computational efficiency and stable optimization. DAMCHA serves as a drop-in replacement for MHA and its variants in various Transformer architectures, delivering superior expressive power and learning performance with comparable or reduced computational overhead. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DAMCHA-based Transformers consistently outperform competing baselines across a range of benchmark tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/erwinmsmith/DAMCHA.

</details>


### [199] [CopRoute: Dependence-Aware Conformal Routing for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13276)
*Siva Rajesh Kasa,Santhosh Kumar Kasa,Karan Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Set-based language model routing aims to increase the coverage of the event where at least one model answers a query correctly while controlling the number of inference calls made. Existing risk-controlled routing policies bound the all-miss risk (the probability that every model in the selected set fails) by calibrating a single conformal threshold and applying it to a nested family of model sets. Because each model is scored in isolation, these policies ignore the dependence structure of model failures. Models that share training data or architecture tend to fail on the same queries, so two individually strong models can be jointly redundant while a weaker but complementary model covers the queries that the others miss. To account for this dependence, which existing risk-controlled policies ignore, we propose CopRoute, which orders models by how many new queries each one answers correctly beyond those already selected, decides per query how far down that order to go, and then picks the smallest cutoff that still meets the target risk. We show that CopRoute's ordering attains, on the design data, the best guarantee any efficient fixed-ordering algorithm can give for the average number of calls to the first correct model, and that a separate calibration step certifies the all-miss risk of the deployed sets. On RouterBench and RouterEval, CopRoute attains the fewest certified calls on nine of ten datasets at a target risk of 0.05 and on eight of ten at 0.10, making up to 79 percent fewer calls (median 24 percent) than the state-of-the-art risk-controlled policy at the same certified all-miss risk.

</details>


### [200] [MHR-Router: Multimodal Heterogeneous Retrieval-Enhanced Adaptive Routing for VLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13278)
*Wenjuan Zhu,Zheming Yang,Yunqing Hu,Qi Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit complementary capabilities, yet selecting the most suitable expert for a given image-question query remains challenging due to heterogeneous multimodal inputs and varying expert strengths. Existing routing approaches rely on text-based cues or static model profiles, failing to capture fine-grained visual evidence and expert-specific failure patterns. To address this, we propose MHR-Router, a multimodal heterogeneous retrieval-enhanced adaptive routing framework for VLM inference. It accurately dispatches each multimodal query to the most suitable VLM expert via local retrieval evidence. MHR-Router consists of two stages: (1) Multimodal Routing Representation, which fuses frozen multimodal features into a comprehensive query representation; and (2) Heterogeneous Retrieval-Enhanced Adaptive Routing, which retrieves expert-specific success and failure memories as local evidence and employs a shared scorer to adaptively select the optimal expert. Experiments show that MHR-Router effectively achieves heterogeneous modality routing and consistently improves accuracy over traditional baselines.

</details>


### [201] [Recovering Irreducible Three-Body Interactions with Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13292)
*Sena Kalabalik,Sukru Caglar,Abdullah Umut Hamzaogullari,Arkadas Ozakin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) learn mechanical systems from trajectory data by parameterizing a scalar Lagrangian and deriving the dynamics through the Euler-Lagrange equations, biasing the model toward motion that respects the variational structure of mechanics. The interactions studied so far have been pairwise-additive, leaving open whether LNNs can recover an irreducible many-body interaction from motion alone. We address this using the Axilrod-Teller triple-dipole interaction, the leading three-body dispersion term, whose dependence on the collective geometry of three particles cannot be represented by any sum of pairwise terms. We prescribe the kinetic energy exactly and learn only the potential, as a function of interparticle distances and angles. Combined with a signal-aware sampling strategy that concentrates training data where the three-body signal is strongest while preserving broad coverage of configuration space, this recovers the interaction from accelerations alone. The learned potential reproduces the radial scaling, angular dependence, and characteristic sign reversal of the Axilrod-Teller interaction, and generates stable trajectories. Embedding it within a pairwise Lennard-Jones potential lets us examine its observability: the three-body term is recovered once its dynamical signature exceeds the residual modeling error, which it does cleanly at amplified coupling but not at the coupling of a real noble gas, bracketing the transition between the two. Finally, symbolic regression on the trained potential, with no assumed functional form, returns the Axilrod-Teller expression with angular numerator, radial law, and coupling constant intact. An LNN with domain-specific structure can thus recover an irreducible three-body interaction from trajectories as a closed-form law, pointing toward learning interaction laws whose structure is not known a priori.

</details>


### [202] [Probabilistic Ship Trajectory Forecasting using Transformer Models on AIS Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13557)
*Frederik E. T. Schöller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Anticipation of vessel trajectories is an essential task when navigating at sea. This applies to both human navigators and autonomous surface vessels. Trajectory forecasting enables sub-routines such as collision avoidance to assess the situation and react accordingly in time. This paper investigates Transformer and Performer models for trajectory prediction of surface vessels in confined waters. Using widely available position data from the Automatic Identification System (AIS), candidate models are trained and evaluated. Historical information on how other vessels have moved in the local area surrounding each agent is conveyed using positional heat maps. The heat maps are generated using Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) and encoded with a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) before entering the Transformer models. The models are trained with quantile loss to efficiently obtain a model-aware prediction interval, as this is crucial for timely collision avoidance. The proposed model architectures are shown to greatly outperform previous work while the inclusion of local heat maps enables the prediction of vessel movement that is not otherwise indicated from the previous positions.

</details>


### [203] [DAG Structure Learning with Node-Varying Heavy-Tailed Disturbances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13304)
*Robin Francis,Sundeep Prabhakar Chepuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: DAG structures can be learned from observations by incorporating acyclicity via smooth constraints, such as DAGMA's log-determinant function. Typically, observations and the underlying DAG are related through linear structural equation models, with Gaussianity assumption on the disturbances at each node. Extending DAG learning methods that account for scale and tail behavior of disturbances that vary across nodes remains a challenge. We propose a node-varying Student-$t$ DAGMA that jointly estimates the graph structure, node-specific disturbance scales, and the degrees-of-freedom parameter. A Gaussian scale-mixture representation yields an iteratively reweighted formulation that down-weights large standardized residuals. Building on existing results, we also establish identifiability of the graph and disturbance parameters. Synthetic experiments demonstrate improved structural recovery under heterogeneous heavy-tailed disturbances, and experiments on real finance data show that the proposed method yields lower test negative log-likelihood than the evaluated baselines.

</details>


### [204] [MORAD: Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Objective Rewards for RNA Inverse Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13305)
*Jixin Tang,Ji Guo,Jun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: We introduce MORAD, an online reinforcement learning framework for backbone-conditioned RNA diffusion models that trains a single shared policy across diverse target backbones. Structure-conditioned RNA inverse design seeks nucleotide sequences compatible with a target three-dimensional backbone, yet existing reinforcement learning approaches either optimize a separate policy for each target backbone or rely on offline preference optimization, leaving online post-training of a reusable backbone-conditioned model largely unexplored. MORAD combines backbone-conditioned group sampling, group-relative reward normalization, and a forward-process diffusion objective. Beyond existing RL rewards that primarily target tertiary structural quality, MORAD jointly optimizes six measurements spanning tertiary geometry, base pairing, ensemble behavior, and sequence composition. To aggregate these heterogeneous objectives despite differences in scale and optimization direction, MORAD maps each measurement to a bounded desirability score and combines them with a weighted geometric mean. Compared with a tertiary-structure-only reward, this multi-objective formulation improves 10 of the 11 evaluation metrics that have a preferred direction. Applied to pretrained RIDE, MORAD improves GDT-TS by 16.0% (from 0.3300 to 0.3827) and pairing MCC by 19.4% (from 0.6110 to 0.7295), while reducing normalized ensemble defect by 27.5% (from 0.3906 to 0.2830). On the test set, MORAD outperforms RiboDiffusion, gRNAde, and RDesign across tertiary structural accuracy, secondary-structure pairing, and thermodynamic quality, establishing a new state of the art in structure-conditioned RNA inverse design.

</details>


### [205] [Structure-Aware Numerical Stabilization for Half-Precision Proximal Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13306)
*Linxuan Wu,Liang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Local online training on resource-constrained platforms requires low computational and training-state overhead. We study the numerical stability and computational efficiency of half-precision proximal policy optimization (PPO) on commodity GPUs. Directly converting PPO training to FP16 is unreliable because numerical errors feed back through policy updates and subsequent data collection, while PPO repeatedly compares a current policy with a fixed behavior policy. We propose PPO-FP16 with three structure-aware modules. Global scalar protection stabilizes statistics shared across a minibatch or parameter update. Policy-change residual protection extracts and preserves small differences between the current and behavior policies. Efficient persistent-update protection stabilizes optimizer state and parameter writeback while reducing state storage. These changes preserve the PPO objective, hyperparameters, and training budget. Across six continuous-control tasks and five seeds, PPO-FP16 achieves the lowest mean return-AUC rank (1.90) among the four methods and ranks first in 17 of 30 environment-seed comparisons. Eight of nine performance configurations yield PPO update speedups ranging from 1.16 to 2.03 times. Relative to Full FP32 Adam, optimizer-state memory is reduced by 71.7 to 74.9 percent, and peak allocated GPU memory is reduced by up to 44.5 percent. The results show that protecting a small set of structurally important quantities can deliver stable, efficient FP16 PPO without retuning.

</details>


### [206] [Sustainable Cloud-Fog-Edge Systems for Smart Buildings: Algorithmic Adaptation under Memory Constraints and Dynamic Orchestration Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13307)
*Mamadou Sow,Christophe Cerin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: The escalating environmental footprint of the Information and Communication Technol- ogy (ICT) sectorwith global Data Center electric- ity usage potentially reaching 3% by 2030 and the imperative for real-time responsiveness in smart buildings demand a foundational shift towards Edge Computing. This article proposes a holis- tic architectural and algorithmic approach for cre- ating autonomous, resilient, and privacy-preserving learning systems within smart buildings, advancing the core concept of the building itself as the data center. Our contributions, demonstrating originality compared to conventional centralized systems, are structured around three main pillars: 1) Validation of resilient data acquisition chains using the af- fordable Arduino ecosystem (Uno R4 WiFi/BME280) and secure persistence solutions including centralized archiving (Node-RED/MySQL) and secure remote transfer (Python/rsync/SSH). 2). The proposal of a lightweight, KOptim orchestration framework, inter- operable with the FIWARE standard, for the dynamic management of Service Level Agreements (SLA). 3) The adaptation and experimental valida- tion of the Generalized Hebbian Algorithm (GHA) for Principal Component Analysis (PCA) on low- power microcontrollers,We introduce and compare an iterative learning strategy using data buffers, featuring sorting and explicit preservation of extreme samples to maintain diversity. Experimental results on the high-dimensional Tour Perret sensor data confirm that this novel method, despite introducing a necessary temporal complexity of O(W log W ) due to sorting, achieves convergence and final result quality that is visually equivalent to processing the entire dataset in full memory, reliably identifying two distinct operational states.

</details>


### [207] [SBNO : Schrödinger Bridge Neural Operator for the Forward and Inverse Problems under Missing Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13317)
*Jaehyeon Park,Inhyeok Jeong,Noseong Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Partial differential equation (PDE) surrogate modeling often relies on either dense observations or access to governing equations/physics constraints. In practice, observations can be sparse and partial, and the underlying physics may be partially specified or unavailable. We introduce SBNO, an equation-free paired-data diffusion bridge matching framework that learns conditional operators for both forward and inverse PDE problems under partial observations. SBNO uses diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching to learn a pair of time-dependent drifts from a mask-aware endpoint coupling that preserves empirical input-solution pairing during training and supports both PDE-task directions within a single framework. At inference time, SBNO produces a deterministic point reconstruction by integrating the probability-flow ODE constructed from the learned drift pair, without inference-time observation or PDE guidance-weight tuning. On five PDE benchmarks with 5% observations, SBNO achieves superior or comparable accuracy to baselines while demonstrating lower test-instance error dispersion. Furthermore, SBNO is at least $9.7 \times$ faster and reduces GPU memory by at least $4.3 \times$ compared to guided-diffusion baselines.

</details>


### [208] [DuoRate: Two-Rate Per-Expert Precision Allocation for Mixture-of-Experts Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13328)
*Mengqi Guo,Zhe Wei,Yuan Yuan,Jiunn Bin Lim,Boyi Pan,Michael Bi Mi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Serving a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model pays for expert precision twice: the resident footprint pays for every expert the router can reach, while each batch-one decode step pays only for the experts it selects. These are two different rate functionals over the same per-expert widths, yet published per-expert MoE allocators constrain only the first. We call the difference the store-read gap; in the released BitsMoE checkpoint, 2.54 bits per weight (bpw) of storage become 7.50 bpw of decode traffic. We propose DuoRate, which stores once and reads per workload. Offline, it stores one uniform tier of a prefix-readable weight stream and bakes a distortion tensor. Online, it profiles a workload's own prefill without labels and solves a Lagrangian rate-distortion allocation under the decode functional; routing frequency provably cancels from every per-expert decision, and the solve costs one dot product per expert and a bisection. A deployment therefore keeps a single artifact that prefill reads at the stored rate and decode reads at the workload's own rate. On Qwen3-30B-A3B, DuoRate reading 2.3 bpw exceeds every 2-bit-class baseline by 6.3 to 47.0 points of average accuracy, and at 2.9 bpw it matches 4-bit formats that read 1.4-1.6 bpw more. The advantage is largest under long decoding: on GSM8K, DuoRate at 2.9 bpw matches or exceeds every 4-bit format and trails BF16 by only 0.25 points, and at 2.6 bpw it still matches or exceeds the 4.5-bit NF4 and FP4. Against controls in its own format, the allocation alone is worth 0.07-0.11 bpw of read rate. Code will be released upon publication.

</details>


### [209] [PDE-PFN: Prior-Data Fitted Neural PDE Solver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13334)
*Jaehyeon Park,Mingu Kang,Dongseok Lee,Woojin Cho,Kookjin Lee,Anthony Gruber,Youngjoon Hong,Noseong Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Motivated by the success of large language models (LLMs) with broad generalizability and robustness to noisy or unreliable pre-training data, we explore in-context prediction of PDE solution fields using approximate pre-training data. In addition, inspired by the posterior predictive mean-based inference mechanism of the in-context learning in prior-data fitted networks (PFNs), we propose PDE-PFN, a prior-data fitted neural solver that approximates the posterior predictive mean of PDE solutions via in-context learning. PDE-PFN builds on a PFN architecture with self- and cross-attention mechanisms of Transformer and is pre-trained on noisy approximate solutions generated by physics-informed neural networks, serving as diverse but not necessarily exact priors. Through experiments on a range of two-dimensional PDEs, we demonstrate that PDE-PFN achieves empirical generalization across heterogeneous equations, robustness under noisy priors, and zero-gradient-update in-context inference capability. PDE-PFN achieves competitive or improved predictive accuracy relative to the evaluated baselines while supporting flexible context and query representations.

</details>


### [210] [AgentTune: A Unified Training Lifecycle for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13335)
*R E Zera Marveen Lyngkhoi,Abhivansh Gupta,Vidushee Vats,Ram Mohan Rao Kadiyala,Vinay Kumar Sankarapu,Pratinav Seth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Tool-using agents increasingly require a common substrate for building, training, evaluating, distilling, and maintaining agentic systems, yet existing tooling typically addresses these stages in isolation. We present AgentTune, a source-available Python library that provides a unified framework for developing and optimizing tool-using agents. At its core, AgentTune introduces a two-tier EventLog representation that preserves agent trajectories while supporting consistent projections for training, evaluation, and auditing. Building on this substrate, the library provides a shared agent runtime, tool-calling reinforcement learning, agentic distillation, retrieval-augmented generation, decision workflows, evaluation utilities, and a designed path toward self-healing agents. These components are exposed through composable interfaces so that agent strategies, training methods, evaluation procedures, and recovery mechanisms can be combined without requiring separate representations or pipelines. We demonstrate the framework across agentic tasks including legal contract search and domain-specific applications, illustrating its use for trajectory-based optimization, tool-use evaluation, and cross-task agent development. AgentTune is released as a source-available library at https://github.com/Lexsi-Labs/AgentTune

</details>


### [211] [Understanding Mamba-3 Through Oscillatory Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13341)
*Shutian Liang,Yue Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Mamba-3 introduces input-dependent rotations into recurrent state updates, but their contribution beyond state retention remains unclear. We interpret these ro- tations as underdamped second-order dynamics, providing a framework for in- vestigating the role of oscillation in sequence modeling. Controlled comparisons show that underdamped dynamics outperform non-oscillatory damping regimes on state-tracking tasks, while language-model pretraining demonstrates benefits for long-context prediction and length generalization. Interventions in pretrained models further establish the importance of learned phase, and state analysis sug- gests that rotation regulates how retained information combines with incoming up- dates. Taken together, these findings identify underdamped dynamics as a useful mechanism for recurrent computation and suggest that oscillation supports long- context processing by regulating how information accumulates.

</details>


### [212] [Hidden Data Challenges in Clinical Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13350)
*Carolin Cissée,Dennis Fast,Raneen Younis,Alexander Löser,Wolfgang Nejdl,Zahra Ahmadi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Widely reused preprocessing pipelines for electronic health records can encode assumptions about when information became available, what clinical episode a label represents, and what missingness means. These assumptions are rarely audited systematically and may propagate across subsequent studies when preprocessing pipelines are reused or adapted. Using MIMIC-IV as a case study, we identify and quantify violations of three complementary forms of preprocessing alignment, i.e., temporal, semantic, and population alignment, which manifest as temporal leakage, feature-task misalignment, and biased imputation. We show how ambiguities in timestamp semantics, episode definitions, endpoint construction, modality selection, and missingness handling can alter the information available to a model and affect the interpretation of its predictions. Through analyses of MIMIC-IV data and empirical case studies, we demonstrate that such choices may remain largely invisible in aggregate predictive performance while changing the signals on which models rely. We derive practical recommendations for temporally valid, clinically aligned, and reproducible preprocessing of MIMIC-IV and related electronic health record datasets. The code for our experiments, including our proposed preprocessing, is available at: https://github.com/m2l-lab/mimic-iv-preprocess.

</details>


### [213] [Opening the Black Box of Classifier-Free Guidance via Information Bottleneck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13369)
*Jiayang Gao,Tianyi Zheng,Jiayang Zou,Fengxiang Yang,Luyao Fan,Lv Tang,Bo Li,Jia Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is a standard technique for improving conditional generation in diffusion models, yet its activation schedule is usually selected heuristically. In practice, most existing CFG-based methods rely on a pre-defined activation policy throughout inference, while recent dynamic variants often depend on hand-designed schedules without a principled theoretical explanation of when conditional information is most useful. In order to understand how the informational relevance between conditioning inputs and the score function evolves over the course of generation, we explore when guidance should be activated from an information-theoretic perspective. In this work, we formulate the diffusion trajectory as a time-dependent information bottleneck and show that the effectiveness of conditional information is not uniform throughout sampling, but becomes significantly stronger after a critical stage characterized by a Fisher-information balance condition. Motivated by this insight, we propose a training-free dynamic CFG strategy for inference. Rather than prescribing a fixed schedule in advance, our method constructs a stochastic process from conditional and unconditional score statistics and activates guidance when the estimated guidance gain crosses a prescribed threshold. This yields an adaptive activation rule that aligns condition injection with the underlying generation dynamics, while also recovering interval-based guidance as a limiting special case. Extensive experiments across diverse architectures and tasks, demonstrate that our method is effective, robust, and broadly applicable. Beyond empirical improvements, our framework provides an interpretation of dynamic guidance methods and a principled foundation for inference-time control in conditional diffusion models.

</details>


### [214] [BLOKS: Scaling Kernel Solvers with Sketched Block Preconditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13382)
*Giordano Vitale,Giacomo Meanti,Marco Rando,Blanca Ayuso de Dios,Lorenzo Rosasco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) is a theoretically sound algorithm for nonparametric learning, which is also often used to investigate properties of deep learning. However, the exact KRR formulation incurs time and memory costs preventing applicability to large datasets, motivating the development of approximate solvers. These algorithms rely on (i) iterative methods, to avoid the computational burden of direct solutions; (ii) sketching or batching, to reduce the problem size; and (iii) preconditioning, to accelerate convergence. While crucial for fast convergence, preconditioning incurs memory costs that can be managed by lowering the number of free parameters of the model, potentially at the expense of expressivity and hence accuracy. To address this challenge, we propose BLOKS, a Conjugate Gradient-based solver for Nyström-sketched KRR built around a novel preconditioner that combines a block Jacobi approximation with sketching. The BLOKS preconditioner has memory cost independent of both the dataset size and the number of parameters, allowing scaling to large models. We provide a memory-efficient, GPU-accelerated implementation and show that BLOKS achieves excellent accuracy while retaining favorable time/memory complexity. Indeed, on standard large-scale datasets used to benchmark kernel solvers, BLOKS achieves comparable or better performances than state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [215] [PlacePulse: Learning Transferable Place Representations from Structural Geographic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13390)
*Lucía García-Duarte,Miguel Álvarez,Diego Vicente,Javier Pérez Trufero,Javier de la Torre*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Geospatial machine learning often depends on task-specific feature engineering over large collections of sociodemographic, business, and environmental variables. Geospatial foundation models offer reusable representations of place, but leading approaches typically encode dynamic signals, such as mobility and search activity, or visual signals from satellite imagery. Few models represent the static structural composition of places (who lives and works there, which businesses operate there, and which risks they face) as transferable embeddings at fine spatial resolution. We introduce PlacePulse, a structural geospatial foundation model that compresses 572 attributes, expanded to 2,288 inputs through H3 neighborhood aggregates, into 256-dimensional unit-norm embeddings for 1,055,537 H3 resolution-7 cells in the United States. We also introduce a reusable two-axis benchmark that separates intrinsic representation quality from downstream predictive utility across multiple targets and spatial supports. Intrinsically, the embeddings reconstruct standardized inputs with mean $R^2 = 0.853$, use 251 of 256 approximately decorrelated dimensions, and show strong spatial organization, with Moran's $I = 0.93$ on the leading principal component and autocorrelation persisting beyond the input radius. Extrinsically, across 74 held-out targets from 12 external sources and four spatial supports, PlacePulse outperforms a dimensionality-matched feature-selection baseline on targets unrelated to the training inputs and is competitive with Google's Population Dynamics Foundation Model, leading on health, economic, election, economic-mobility and social-vulnerability outcomes, with relative strengths that vary by domain. These findings position structural composition as a distinct and complementary modality for geospatial foundation models, not simply a fallback where behavioral or imagery data are unavailable.

</details>


### [216] [Why Linear Models Can Still Work in a Complex and Changing World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13404)
*Fangzhou Lin,Dengzhe Hou,Yinxing Li,Kazunori D Yamada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Linear models can predict well in complex domains even when underlying mechanisms are nonlinear. We argue that this can occur because the complexity of the world exceeds the complexity that can be learned reliably from finite, partially observed data. Flexible models can exploit curvature, thresholds, and interactions that fit one hospital, period, population, policy setting, or measurement process but fail elsewhere. When this instability lies mainly in the nonlinear component, omitting it can give linear models lower prediction error in a new environment. We connect this claim to work on simple linear rules, bias and variance, regularization, best linear prediction, measurement error, distribution shift, invariance, Rashomon sets, and underspecification. Proposition 1 gives a simple squared-error case in which a nonlinear rule learned in the training environment raises target error because it transfers too weakly; finite-sample estimation error can strengthen this result. Partial observation alone is not enough: the link between observed variables and unobserved factors must vary across environments. Stable nonlinear structure favors flexible models, and linear models remain vulnerable to linear spurious relationships. Model complexity should therefore be chosen by cross-environment stability as well as training-data accuracy.

</details>


### [217] [Accelerating Linear Bandits with Persistent Candidate Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13407)
*Bingkui Tong,Daria Korovaitceva,Marina Sheshukova,Sergey Samsonov,Junpei Komiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Exhaustive optimistic scoring can make action selection costly in linear bandits with a large fixed set of $K$ actions, requiring $\mathcal{O}(K)$ score evaluations per round. We introduce Persistent Candidate LinUCB (\textsc{PC-LinUCB}), which maintains $k$ active actions and samples $q$ challengers uniformly from the remaining $K-k$ actions at each round. By scoring only these $k+q$ candidates, \textsc{PC-LinUCB} reduces the number of score evaluations per round to $\mathcal{O}(k+q)$. We establish problem-independent and problem-dependent high-probability regret bounds consisting of a standard linear-bandit learning term and an additive discovery cost of order $\mathcal{O}\left((K-k)\log(K-k)/q\right)$ at fixed confidence. Notably, this additional cost does not grow with the horizon. For fixed gaps and sufficiently long horizons, we establish an $Ω\left((K-k)/q\right)$ regret lower bound under uniform challenger sampling, showing that partial candidate access itself imposes an unavoidable regret cost. Experiments show that \textsc{PC-LinUCB} substantially accelerates LinUCB while maintaining comparable regret, and that the persistent candidate mechanism can extend beyond LinUCB.

</details>


### [218] [In-Context Learning for System Identification: Probabilistic Formulation, Long-Sequence Handling, and Benchmark Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13411)
*Matteo Rufolo,Dario Piga,Filippo Pura,Marco Forgione*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Traditional system identification typically involves two steps: initially, training data is processed to estimate a model of the system of interest; then, the model is used to predict outputs for new input queries. In contrast, recent in-context learning approaches learn a meta model that maps directly from training data and query inputs to outputs in a zero-shot fashion, bypassing explicit model estimation. This approach leverages a meta model trained offline, on large sets of synthetically generated data, to emulate the behavior of a system identification algorithm. In this work, we advance this meta-learning framework for system identification in three directions. First, we develop a unified probabilistic meta-identification architecture that combines a Transformer-based encoder-decoder with tailored mechanisms for handling long training sequences, enabling calibrated predictions over long horizons. Second, we show how a pre-trained meta model can act as an uncertainty-aware synthetic data generator for classical system identification, providing task-specific data that regularize parametric models when real data are scarce. Third, we systematically evaluate the framework on standard nonlinear system identification benchmarks and on a challenging out-of-distribution scenario, demonstrating competitive accuracy with state-of-the-art methods and strong generalization, including cases where input-only adaptation is sufficient to handle significant distribution shifts.

</details>


### [219] [PPG-Bench: A Unified Benchmark for PPG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13435)
*Arvasu Kulkarni,Mahir Jain,Aditya Ray Mishra,Sandeep Singh,Siddharth Panwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Photoplethysmography (PPG) is increasingly recorded by wearable and clinical devices at enormous scale, and a growing number of PPG foundation models (FMs) have been developed to compute reusable representations for various downstream tasks. These models are difficult to compare: different evaluations use different datasets, task definitions, preprocessing, downstream heads, hyperparameter tuning, and baselines. We present PPG-Bench, a unifying open-source benchmark evaluating five public PPG FMs across nine open-access datasets and 26 tasks spanning cardiovascular state estimation, activity, sleep, screening, and identification. We evaluate models under four downstream regimes of increasing adaptability (linear probing, random forests, frozen MLP probes, and end-to-end finetuning), with model-specific hyperparameter optimization and subject-disjoint cross-validation. We also evaluate against three controls: random/constant baseline, a deterministic pulse-rate-variability (PRV) feature set, and a generic time-series FM (MOMENT). Across all 68 task-regime combinations, 35.3% show no meaningful FM advantage over the random/constant baseline, and in 83.8% no PPG-specific FM outperforms both PRV and MOMENT. We also find that added downstream head capacity generally improves performance, but can cause significant degradation if the model is performing at chance/constant level. Current PPG FMs therefore cannot yet claim to provide generalised and transferrable representations of PPG signals, and future evaluation in this field should include checks against simple, non-learned controls. Code for the benchmark and instructions to evaluate more models can be found here - https://github.com/mannas-ai/ppg-bench.

</details>
