### [220] [Beyond Temporal Proximity: Explanatory World Models for Credit Assignment in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13504)
*Oriol Corcoll,Qiyao Wei,Ciarán Mark Gilligan-Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Generative Recommenders increasingly use Reinforcement Learning (RL) to optimize outcomes that may appear hundreds or thousands of interactions after a decision. Standard RL propagates reward according to temporal proximity rather than explicit relevance, attenuating the signal assigned to distant but important actions and creating a source of sample inefficiency. We introduce Explanatory World Models (EWM), an attribution framework that uses explanations to identify the earlier events that plausibly contributed to a realized outcome. We instantiate EWM with a frontier language model and use its explanations as learning signals using a conservative hierarchical operator that recursively redistributes reward while preserving the undiscounted episode return. To investigate what constitutes a useful explanation, we operationalize three complementary variants inspired by Halpern's actual causation, Lombrozo's explanatory simplicity, and Deutsch's hard-to-vary explanations. We evaluate EWM on two controlled long-horizon RL benchmarks, a public sequential-recommendation benchmark, and an industry-scale generative recommender. On Crafter and WordCraft, EWM substantially outperforms PPO and LaRe, with its largest gains when rewards are most delayed. On KuaiRand, EWM outperforms SFT and LaRe offline and A2C online. Finally, in an offline evaluation of an industry generative recommender, EWM considerably improves Recall and increases behavioral proxies. These results suggest that explanations can provide structured and reusable credit for learning from delayed outcomes.

</details>


### [221] [FedPCD: Priority-Constrained Federated Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13508)
*Yousef Irshaid,Dara Varam,Mohamed I. AlHajri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) seeks to optimize a shared global model from decentralized client data. While effective for average performance, this process can produce unfair performance across clients, particularly under statistically heterogeneous data distributions. Existing fairness-aware and multi-objective FL methods address this through client re-weighting, gradient correction, or common-descent formulations, but often change the global optimization direction or treat client objectives symmetrically. We propose FedPCD, a priority-constrained federated optimization framework that separates the global optimization objective from client protection. FedPCD preserves the conventional federated aggregate as the primary objective, while allowing only the minimum correction necessary to meet client performance constraints. Across heterogeneous FL benchmarks, FedPCD consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving the highest global accuracy across all configurations and leading worst-10% client accuracy in nearly all. FedPCD further preserves competitive trade-offs between global accuracy and client performance variability. Beyond this trade-off, FedPCD demonstrates faster empirical convergence, reaching matching or superior performance in substantially fewer communication rounds.

</details>


### [222] [Simulating Training Spikes of Deep Neural Networks by Layer-wise Random Feature Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13524)
*Ran Tian,Courtney Paquette*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Intermittent ``spikes'' in loss, during the pre-training of Large Language Models (LLMs), remains a mysterious phenomenon both theoretically and empirically. In this paper, we show that simple quadratic models with heavy-tailed, power-law distributed inputs can replicate these spikes. We introduce a theoretical framework and simulation algorithm to model per-layer training dynamics of Deep Neural Networks (DNNs), using such quadratic models. In a case study on an Adam hyperparameter configuration (where $β_2$ is set close to $1$) known to induce spikes in Transformer LMs, our simulation successfully predicts bumps indicating spiky behavior from a given checkpoint. Furthermore, we derive an appropriate $β_2$ schedule from observed metrics, which empirically improves performance.

</details>


### [223] [Rationally Linearized MLPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13568)
*Arijit Sehanobish,Byeongchan Kim,Avinava Dubey,Min-hwan Oh,Krzysztof Choromanski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: We present Rationally Linearized Multilayer Perceptrons(RaLiMPs), a new approach to efficiently linearizing MLPs using low-degree rational approximations of standard activation functions. Inspired by polynomial-based MLP linearization, we show that rational approximations can provide accurate representations at substantially lower degree. RaLiMPs exploit these approximations to construct kernel representations with narrow bottlenecks, enabling efficient composition across MLP layers. We provide theoretical guarantees for the resulting approximation and evaluate RaLiMPs across applications ranging from implicit 3D models to large language models. Our experiments demonstrate favorable accuracy--efficiency tradeoffs, with RaLiMPs achieving competitive or improved accuracy while substantially reducing inference cost.

</details>


### [224] [Simultaneous Competitive and Regret Guarantees for TCP Acknowledgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13571)
*Tsubasa Harada,Yasushi Kawase,Hanna Sumita*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: We study simultaneous competitive and regret guarantees for TCP acknowledgment. Each acknowledgment serves all pending packets, and the objective is to minimize the total acknowledgment and waiting costs. It is well known that no deterministic online algorithm achieves a competitive ratio better than $2$. Given $k$ randomized online experts and $0<\varepsilon<1$, we construct a randomized online algorithm whose realized total cost is always at most $(2+\varepsilon)$ times the offline optimal cost $\mathrm{OPT}$ and whose expected regret against the expert with the smallest realized cost is $O(\sqrt{\mathrm{OPT}\log(k+1)}+\log(1/\varepsilon))$. The algorithm requires no advance knowledge of the number of packets or $\mathrm{OPT}$. Our algorithm controls switching costs between experts and uses the gap between the permitted competitive bound and its current cost to determine when to learn. We also prove lower bounds showing that, for $k\ge 2$, this regret bound is asymptotically optimal in $\mathrm{OPT}$, $k$, and $\varepsilon$, and that $\varepsilon>0$ is necessary for sublinear regret.

</details>


### [225] [$L_0$ Feature Selection via Monte Carlo Tree Search in High-Dimensional Financial Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13574)
*Cameron Mouangue,Tristan Cazenave*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Feature selection is a central problem in high-dimensional financial regression, where large numbers of engineered variables and strong correlations among predictors reduce model stability and interpretability. $L_1$ regularization (Lasso) is widely used for sparse regression, but its shrinkage can be restrictive when informative signals are spread across correlated variables. Exact sparse regression avoids this shrinkage but requires solving a combinatorial optimization problem that is computationally difficult and is typically fit to minimize in-sample rather than out-of-sample error. This paper presents a feature selection framework based on Monte Carlo Tree Search with Generalized Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation (GNRPA). Feature selection is formulated as a sequential decision process, and candidate subsets are scored by their out-of-sample validation performance rather than training error. The method also supports a lexicographic objective accounting for subset size and multicollinearity. We study this approach on a contemporaneous explanatory regression task: explaining the weekly return of the NASDAQ-100 index from 1,072 engineered features spanning market, macroeconomic, and factor data. This isolates the value of subset selection under multicollinearity from the separate problem of predictive forecasting. On this task, the proposed method identifies sparse feature subsets with higher out-of-sample explanatory power than an optimized Lasso baseline, reaching an out-of-sample $R^2$ of 0.6961 versus 0.6313 for Lasso. These results suggest that Monte Carlo Tree Search is a practical alternative to convex regularization for sparse explanatory feature selection in financial regression, with potential value for factor attribution and risk decomposition where transparency matters as much as out-of-sample fit.

</details>


### [226] [EchoTab: Learning Measurement-Grounded Representations for Echocardiographic Diagnosis through Tabular Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13599)
*Michelle Espranita Liman,Ioana Ionac,Alexander Müller,Eimo Martens,Daniel Rueckert,Philip Müller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Diagnostic information in multi-view medical imaging studies, like echocardiography, is distributed across views, each capturing different aspects of cardiac structure and function. In clinical practice, these views are first interpreted through quantitative measurements, which then support specific diagnoses. However, existing echo representation learning methods do not explicitly model measurements as an intermediate representation between views and diagnosis. As a result, individual measurements are not explicitly represented in a form that downstream diagnostic tasks can selectively use, hindering accurate diagnosis.
  Inspired by this hierarchical clinical workflow, we introduce EchoTab, a measurement-grounded representation learning framework that represents multi-view echocardiography studies using measurement-specific tokens as an intermediate representation between echo views and diagnosis. Each measurement token selectively attends to the views relevant to its estimation and is trained through tabular reconstruction to predict the corresponding measurement value. The resulting tokens form a factorized echo representation, which encode clinically meaningful structural and functional properties and can be selectively used for downstream diagnosis. Unlike the commonly used contrastive approaches, EchoTab does not require negative samples or large batch sizes. Experiments on two datasets demonstrate that EchoTab learns effective measurement-grounded representations that improve diagnosis classification in multi-view echocardiography and its learned attention aligns with established measurement-view relationships used by cardiologists. Our code is accessible at https://github.com/michelleespranita/EchoTab.

</details>


### [227] [A small cognitive core explains human behavior at scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13603)
*Marcel Binz,Eric Schulz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Psychology has proposed many principles of cognition. Each, however, was developed in isolation, leaving their combined explanatory power unknown. We assembled a small set of established principles into one model, which we call COntextual Retrieval and Encoding (CORE). CORE consists of a hierarchy of episodic memories into which prediction errors are written and whose encoding, retrieval, and forgetting depend on context. Fitted jointly to 7,036,442 choices from 137 experiments, the model predicts human choices better than black-box models and reproduces 75% of reported behavioral effects when simulated. Moreover, its latent variables show classic signatures of Bayesian learning and event segmentation. Taken together, these findings suggest that a small cognitive core accounts for much of human behavior. They also open a path toward making psychology more than the sum of its parts, by building computational models on the entire experimental record.

</details>


### [228] [Private and Distributionally Robust Federated Multiclass Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13631)
*Andrea P. Hernandez,Michael Ibrahim,Nagi Gebraeel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Fault diagnosis in asset-intensive industries requires multiclass models trained on noisy, sensitive data collected across geographically distributed client sites. Federated learning enables collaborative training without centralizing data, but most approaches do not provide formal privacy guarantees for individual records stored at client sites and can degrade under label noise. To address these limitations, we develop a differentially private, distributionally robust framework for federated multiclass classification that provides sample-level privacy for records stored at each client. We build on a federated Wasserstein distributionally robust multiclass objective with the Crammer-Singer loss, solved using an Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM)-based algorithm. To protect the released client updates, our method injects calibrated Gaussian noise into the proximal linearized client updates of both the local model and the local distributionally robust variable before aggregation at the central server, and we quantify the cumulative privacy loss using the moments accountant. We provide per-round sample-level $(ε,δ)$-differential privacy guarantees and an overall composed privacy guarantee across all training rounds, together with convergence results and an explicit privacy-utility tradeoff. Experiments on simulated industrial fault data and UCI benchmark datasets show that our method outperforms differentially private federated empirical risk minimization and stochastic gradient descent baselines in most tested settings, with the largest gains under strong privacy-induced noise.

</details>


### [229] [PANDA: Prior-guided Attentional Dual-path Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13635)
*Rishabh Jain,Federico Belotti,Stefano Coniglio,Pietro Lio,Stefano Fiorini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Hypergraph Neural Networks (HNNs), designed to model higher-order relations beyond pairwise interactions, typically rely either on fixed structural weights or on normalised attention. Fixed weights can propagate misleading information in heterophilic settings, while normalisation makes attention coefficients competitive, in the sense that, in a message-passing framework, increasing the weight of one sender must reduce the weight of others in the same aggregation set. Our main contribution is the introduction of PANDA (Prior-guided AttentioNal Dual-path Architecture), a plug-in for two-stage message passing HNNs applicable to both undirected and directed settings. The main path interpolates between learned receiver-normalised attention and structural incidence priors, while a non-competitive (not normalised) auxiliary path lets each sender contribute its own transformed information. We also show that, with fixed structural coefficients, PANDA recovers a certain complex-valued convolution operator adopted in previous works. Across four HNNs spanning spatial and spectral methods in both directed and undirected settings, plugging PANDA in yields an average improvement of 5.18 percentage points across eight real-world benchmark datasets. The code is available at https://github.com/jainris/panda.

</details>


### [230] [SelectArena: A Modern Benchmark for Feature Selection on Real-world Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13644)
*Bastian Schäfer,Dominika Matus,Lennart Purucker,Ricardo Knauer,Erik Rodner,Frank Hutter,Anton Sokolov,Arnaud Liefooghe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Selecting a subset of features (columns) from a tabular dataset is a ubiquitous challenge in real-world applications. Industry experts seek to minimize model training costs, climate scientists require an interpretable subset of factors to understand extreme weather events, and clinicians need to choose among diagnostic tests due to financial and time constraints. When looking at the scientific literature to determine which feature selection method to use, practitioners across domains face an unrepresentative landscape of benchmarking. Despite extensive research, existing feature selection benchmarks provide limited guidance because they rely on outdated models, ad hoc evaluation protocols, or artificially generated data that do not reflect real-world problems. To close the gap between scientific benchmarking and real-world applications, we introduce SelectArena, a modern benchmark for feature selection. We compare 15 widely used feature selection methods on 71 manually curated real-world tabular datasets, covering classification and regression problems, using standardized evaluation protocols and state-of-the-art tabular (foundation) models. Our results reveal clear trade-offs across predictive performance, runtime, stability, and validity. Tree-based methods, CART and RFImportance, along with F-Test are Pareto-optimal when taking into account predictive performance and runtime. F-Test, information-theory-, and distance-based methods lead on stability and validity, with F-Test emerging as a balanced choice across our metrics. Search-based methods are slow, unstable and achieve low performance. SelectArena turns these findings into practical guidance and, as an extensible platform, supports future feature selection research.

</details>


### [231] [TabReliBench: Evaluating Tabular Foundation Models Beyond Predictive Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13654)
*Tianqi Zhao,Jia Guo,Yan Shuo Tan,Qiong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are increasingly popular for their strong predictive performance. Many also provide posterior predictive distributions (PPDs), whose reliability remains less studied. We assess reliability through point prediction, distributional quality, marginal and subgroup calibration, and interval efficiency. We then introduce TabReliBench, a large classification and regression benchmark for these comparisons and dataset-level analyses. We find that TFMs' rankings change between TabArena and our benchmark and across reliability metrics. Regression TFMs usually achieve near-nominal marginal coverage and retain narrow intervals, but this favorable marginal behavior does not imply valid conditional coverage. Dataset-level analyses show that classification difficulty often accompanies poor calibration, whereas regression predictive performance is not a reliable indicator of calibration. Ensembling improves predictive performance but does not consistently improve calibration of PPDs; TFMs also benefit little from conformal post-processing, which does not reliably resolve subgroup gaps. Reliable TFM selection therefore requires multidimensional evaluation beyond aggregate rankings. Code is available at https://github.com/Tianqi-Zhao/TabReliBench.

</details>


### [232] [Pretraining and Test-Time Training Budgets Matter for ARC: A Controlled Study of VARC and TRM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13660)
*Adrien Deliege,Marc Van Droogenbroeck*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) is increasingly used to evaluate whether neural networks can infer task-specific transformations from few demonstrations. Recent ARC models differ in architecture, but also in broader training and evaluation protocols, including pretraining data, data augmentation, test-time setup, and optimization budgets, making conclusions about architecture benefits debatable. In this work, we study how the performance of the vision-inspired VARC and recursion-based TRM models is affected by pretraining and test-time training (TTT) budgets under a common inductive protocol on ARC-AGI-1. We cross five pretraining budgets with four TTT budgets for each test task. Each budget specifies a task-variant count and total sample presentations, defining the controlled training budgets. We observe that increasing TTT sample presentations improves the standard ARC pass@2 metric in all settings for both models. Increasing the TTT task-variant count consistently helps only TRM, whereas VARC often performs better with fewer variants. VARC also remains competitive without pretraining, while TRM benefits much more from it. These results show that pretraining and TTT budgets affect ARC evaluation and motivate reporting at comparable budgets.

</details>


### [233] [On the Encoding of Calendar Features in Covariate-Informed Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13676)
*Alexander Kreusel,Maximilian Beichter,Alexandra Nikoltchovska,Benjamin Schäfer,Ralf Mikut,Frederik Zahn,Veit Hagenmeyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) increasingly offer covariate support, with calendar information being the most common type of categorical covariate in practice, as it is available for every point in time. However, how these features are encoded is largely treated as an implementation detail rather than as an object of study, despite encoding being one of the few design choices left for pretrained models at inference time. With the present study, we demonstrate that the way information is propagated into the model substantially affects forecast accuracy of TSFMs. Therefore, we systematically compare seven calendar encodings, including three conventional target-agnostic representations (ordinal, one-hot, and cyclic) and four target encodings, across four state-of-the-art TSFMs on nine datasets from multiple domains in a zero-shot rolling evaluation. The nine datasets are influenced by social activities, such as weekends and holidays, making calendar information particularly relevant for forecasting. We find that encoding choice affects forecast accuracy across all models, with MASE differences of up to 9.9 % on non-holidays and 83 % on holidays within a single model. Effectiveness and utilization vary substantially across models. Improvements are concentrated around holidays, where calendar information provides signals unavailable from target history, while some model encoding combinations also improve non-holiday forecasts. A SHAP-based attribution analysis locates the contribution of the covariates relative to the autoregressive component and explains why some model-encoding combinations degrade performance. Our findings suggest that current benchmarks for covariate-informed TSFMs can distort model comparisons by imposing fixed encoding strategies.

</details>


### [234] [VQIL: Visible Quantum Interference Learning for Perturbation Response Ranking on Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13689)
*Seokjun Lee,Sergio A. Ortega,Daniel K. Park,Namshik Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Learning how local perturbations affect a network is important for identifying which components drive or absorb a system's response. We formulate this as seed-conditioned response ranking: given a perturbation at one seed node, ranking all other nodes by response strength. This is challenging when the model is given only the interaction topology, while the mechanism governing how perturbations propagate must be learned from data. We present VQIL (Visible Quantum Interference Learning), a trainable Szegedy quantum walk for seed-conditioned ranking. Edge weights, link phases, and per-node phase rotations are optimized end-to-end by backpropagation while the walk operator remains unitary throughout training. Node scores are obtained from a geometrically discounted coherent sum of quantum-walk amplitudes, enabling quantum interference before the final squared-norm readout. Because each learned parameter is attached to a named edge or node, the trained model provides directly inspectable hypotheses about the roles of individual graph components in the propagation mechanism. On AC power-flow perturbations over six transmission grids, with electrical parameters withheld from the model, VQIL improves Spearman rank correlation from 0.147 for the untrained walk to 0.831 and outperforms an identically trained random-walk model. On gene perturbation over five protein-protein interaction networks with Perturb-seq supervision, VQIL improves rank correlation from 0.171 to 0.620, outperforming the strongest learned classical and neural baselines. These results show that trainable quantum-walk interference provides a scalable and interpretable inductive bias for learning seed-conditioned response rankings on graphs.

</details>


### [235] [Transformer-based In-Context Score Operator Learning for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13690)
*Wuzhe Xu,Zhaiming Shen,Yulong Lu,Wenjing Liao,Rongjie Lai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Diffusion models typically learn the generative dynamics of a single target distribution or a family of distributions indexed by conditioning variables fixed during training. This limits their ability to adapt to a previously unseen distribution from only a small set of examples. We introduce an in-context solution operator learning framework for diffusion models, in which each target distribution is treated as a task drawn from a meta-distribution and is specified at inference time by an empirical context set. Our method learns an in-context score operator that maps the context samples, a noisy query point, and the diffusion time to the task-dependent score, enabling reverse-diffusion sampling on unseen tasks without retraining or parameter updates. A key observation is that the task-dependent score admits a Gaussian-kernel conditional-mean representation. Its empirical Nadaraya--Watson approximation can be represented exactly by a model in our constructed transformer class, providing a principled foundation for the proposed query-dependent attention architecture. The operator is trained through amortized denoising score matching across task distributions. We establish generalization-error bounds that quantify the effects of the context size, the numbers of training tasks and within-task samples, model complexity, and intrinsic and ambient dimensions. Numerically, we evaluate the method on a range of scientific and visual datasets, including Navier-Stokes, ERA5 climate data, QuickDraw sketches, and Food-101. The results show that the proposed method performs well on relatively simple task families, while its performance degrades as task complexity increases, thereby illustrating both the promise and the limitations of in-context learning for diffusion models.

</details>


### [236] [Variance-Reduced Policy Gradient is Nearly Minimax-Optimal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13692)
*Leonardo Cesani,Matteo Papini,Marcello Restelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Standard Policy Gradient (PG) algorithms suffer from high estimation variance because they rely on Monte Carlo updates. While off-policy variance reduction mitigates this problem, establishing optimal sample complexity currently requires either restrictive assumptions, such as uniformly bounded importance weights, or impractical second-order operations. In this work, we introduce Loopless Variance-Reduced PG (LVR-PG), a unified theoretical framework that abstracts a class of first-order estimators that integrate recursive updates with defensive importance sampling, including known estimators such as PAGE-PG and STORM-PG. We prove that this algorithmic class achieves $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(ε^{-2})$ last-iterate global convergence under weak gradient domination for general parametric policies. We establish this guarantee using purely first-order updates, with minimal assumptions and without gradient preconditioners. By extending state-of-the-art lower bounds to continuous parametric policies, we establish an $Ω(ε^{-2})$ sample complexity limit, confirming our framework is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors and horizon terms.

</details>


### [237] [Calibration in One Go: Jointly Learning Accurate and Calibrated Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13693)
*Nektarios Kalampalikis,Pablo M. Olmos,Isabel Valera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Neural networks (NNs), while highly flexible and versatile, are often poorly calibrated. Post-hoc calibration methods adjust the outputs of an already trained model using a separate calibration dataset and additional calibration parameters. This process adds overhead that requires separate optimization of the calibrator and subsequent model selection. In this paper, we show that standard (post-hoc) calibrators can be trained end-to-end with the backbone via a bilevel Stackelberg formulation. This formulation eliminates the need for a separate calibration set or a dedicated calibration loss by jointly optimizing accuracy and calibration using cross-entropy, introducing only one additional hyperparameter: the calibrator learning rate. Although our framework supports calibrators ranging from temperature and matrix scaling to NN layers, our results show that using the final classification layer itself as the calibrator yields accurate, well-calibrated models with no additional parameters. Our approach outperforms SOTA calibrators with minimal added complexity, while also avoiding the multiplicative overhead of separate calibration components in settings such as multi-task learning.

</details>


### [238] [Towards Understanding the Training of GFlowNets: A Theoretical Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13711)
*Rui Hu,Yu Chen,Zhuoran Li,Longbo Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) learn policies that sample discrete objects in proportion to a positive reward. They are trained by minimizing balance objectives, whose exact satisfaction guarantees correct sampling. However, a comprehensive theory linking nonzero balance loss and approximate stationarity to sampling accuracy, especially under evolving sampling distributions and backward policies, is still lacking. To bridge this gap, we develop a unified theory for Flow Matching, Detailed Balance, and Trajectory Balance in this regime. We first bound terminal symmetric KL by the square root of the expected balance loss. Then, we show that approximate stationarity controls this distribution error up to a tangent-space residual measuring parameterization incompleteness. Finally, we establish finite-time convergence under fixed or controlled sampling and fixed, balance-trained, or separately updated backward policies. Under standard smoothness assumptions, with fixed or controlled sampling and a fixed or balance-trained backward policy, the expected loss is $O(\barε^2+T^{-1})$ and expected terminal symmetric KL is $O(\barε+T^{-1/2})$ under uniform coverage. For a separately updated backward policy, we characterize the sufficient update decay to approach the residual neighborhood and preserve these rates. These results connect balance objectives to sampling accuracy and clarify how parameterization, coverage, and moving components govern GFlowNet training.

</details>


### [239] [Machine Unlearning for Federated Socially Fair k-Means Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13717)
*Aravind Reddy,Deeraj S K,Mohd Wasif Raza,Saurav Prakash*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Federated clustering can support applications such as patient stratification across hospitals and customer segmentation across distributed financial datasets. Responsible AI calls for fair representation of demographic groups and support for data deletion, motivated in part by rights such as the GDPR's right to erasure. These goals interact: deleting records can change the balance between groups and alter the clustering of the remaining population. We study exact machine unlearning for federated socially fair $k$-means, whose objective is to minimize the largest group-average squared distance to the nearest center. Our approach keeps separate local summaries for each group, so summaries at unaffected institutions remain valid as global fairness weights change. For two groups, we prove an initialization approximation guarantee independent of client group proportions under ideal sampling, and we show that same-seed replay reproduces retraining exactly. Reuse pays off most when a whole client withdraws: across three datasets, replay is $35.8\times$ faster than retraining for the initialization-only model and remains $2.4\times$ and $1.7\times$ faster with one and two fair-refinement rounds, which improve worst-group cost. For individual record deletions, gains are modest (about $1.06\times$), and certifying cached assignments can cost more than it saves. These results map where exact unlearning can profitably reuse computation. Code and reproducibility materials are available at https://github.com/arareddy/mufsc.

</details>


### [240] [Amortized Counterfactual Prediction with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13724)
*Hendrik Stumpf,Francesco Quinzan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Counterfactual inference asks how an observed outcome might have differed under different circumstances, such as whether a patient would have recovered had they received a different treatment. Such questions arise whenever decisions depend on the outcomes of alternatives that were not observed. Answering counterfactual queries generally requires knowledge of the underlying causal structure, which is often unavailable in practice. A common approach is therefore to first estimate the causal graph from observational data and then perform counterfactual inference. However, errors in the estimated causal graph can propagate to downstream counterfactual predictions. To address this challenge, we introduce the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP), an end-to-end approach that answers counterfactual queries directly from observational data without first estimating a causal graph. CFGNP uses Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to exploit the dual-graph structure by restricting cross-world message passing to corresponding factual--counterfactual variables. Across synthetic and image-based benchmarks, CFGNP achieves stronger predictive performance than existing approaches, including on the German Loan benchmark, where it reduces MSE from $4.51 \times 10^{-2}$ to $0.19 \times 10^{-2}$. Furthermore, on synthetic causal graphs, a model trained only on 10-node graphs maintains strong predictive performance on graphs with up to 30 nodes, without retraining.

</details>


### [241] [SeoulMMOD: A Large-Scale Multimodal Origin-Destination Flow Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13730)
*Taeyoung Yu,Seonbin Jo,Jiwon Kim,Junyoung Byun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Origin-destination (OD) flow forecasting supports urban mobility decisions by predicting the volume and patterns of human movement across a city. Most existing public OD benchmarks provide only a partial view of citywide mobility, covering a single transport service or at most two modes. With partial mode coverage, observed demand changes are difficult to separate into shifts across transport modes and changes in total travel. We introduce SeoulMMOD, a large-scale public benchmark for citywide multimodal mobility in Seoul. SeoulMMOD provides three years of hourly OD flows estimating total mobility for 6 urban travel modes across 25 districts and 426 sub-districts, together with travel-time, travel-distance, calendar, rainfall, and point-of-interest (POI) signals. Using SeoulMMOD, we benchmark 14 forecasting baselines under a common protocol and analyse the effects of spatial scaling, joint versus mode-separate training, and cross-year generalization. Results show that several spatio-temporal models that work at district level do not scale to sub-district OD forecasting, and that naive joint multi-mode training does not consistently improve accuracy. Together, the dataset and benchmark establish a reproducible testbed for multi-mode, multi-year OD forecasting at the city scale.

</details>


### [242] [Orthros: Phase-Aware Heterogeneous Attention for Efficient Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13735)
*Yuhong Chou,Zehao Liu,Yuqi Pan,Qian Liu,Xianwei Chen,Zhongzhu Zhou,Jibin Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Efficient attention mechanisms, such as linear and sliding window attention, aim to reduce the computational complexity of Transformers from quadratic to linear. However, their degraded in-context recall often necessitates interleaving full attention layers, leaving the quadratic bottleneck unresolved in long-context processing. To address this, we introduce Orthros attention, which shifts the efficiency paradigm from structural allocation to phase-aware decoupling. As a unified module, it dynamically switches its computation pattern across different phases of sequence processing, enabling linear complexity prefilling and full attention decoding. Our design grants Orthros attention a lower asymptotic complexity compared to vanilla Transformers in prevalent long-context scenarios characterized by heavy prefilling and light decoding. Beyond theoretical formulation and complexity analysis, extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate Orthros's superiority across multiple dimensions. First, we validate its fundamental language modeling capabilities, demonstrating scalability across varying model sizes and strong competitiveness in comprehensive architectural comparisons. Second, Orthros exhibits exceptional conversion feasibility via a data-efficient Transformer-to-Orthros conversion, yielding performance that even surpasses the original Transformer. Finally, Orthros achieves a $21.9\times$ prefilling speedup over Transformers with end-to-end inference gains, while preserving long-context capabilities, as demonstrated by near-perfect 128K needle-in-a-haystack results and Transformer-level performance on real-world in-context recall tasks. Overall, these advantages in high performance, flexibility, and efficiency position Orthros as a promising candidate to succeed the Transformer in industrial applications.

</details>


### [243] [SafeDiSTL: Decomposing Joint Constraints for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13737)
*Hongrui Zheng,Aditya Singh,Rahul Mangharam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent tasks often impose constraints on a shared objective or collective safety. A team level violation cost function can measure this constraint outcome, but does not distinguish which agents' actions most strongly influence that violation. We present SafeDiSTL, a constrained multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that allows for credit assignment even with joint constraint measurements. We use Signal Temporal Logic (STL) robustness to measure episode-level violation through running-maximum cost increments. Smooth full-episode robustness and differentiable dynamics provide action sensitivities, which allocate each cost increment across agents at the same decision time. These sensitivities account for delayed effects when weighting agents' shares of each cost increment. We study the framework in cooperative payload transport, connected navigation, and networked traffic control tasks. SafeDiSTL achieves 1.6-5.1x the safe-success rate in transport, improves navigation safe-success by 11.1%, and delivers 50-68% more throughput in traffic when compared to constrained baselines. Results demonstrate a strong improvement of the learned policy in dealing with shared constraint while retaining the performance of unconstrained policies.

</details>


### [244] [Studying the Effects of Task Diversity Using Small Mathematical Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13750)
*Emily Xia Meng,Xuanyu Yang,Junaid Hasan,Michael Ruofan Zeng,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Basic mathematical tasks like modular addition have been a valuable tool for illuminating aspects of neural network behavior that are challenging to see in noisy, unverifiable, large-scale, real-world settings. These studies have typically focused on a single task. However, modern language models are exposed to a vast number of tasks despite being trained with a next-token prediction objective. In this work, we introduce two mathematical task ecosystems, IntWorld and PermWorld, built from distinct but related tasks on integers and permutations. Both are designed to simulate task diversity within a cohesive mathematical 'world' where tasks are distinct but related. Because the datasets are procedurally generated, we can precisely vary the number of tasks, allowing us to begin to disentangle the role of task variety from other factors in training. After describing the datasets, we provide some initial results obtained from these datasets which support the idea that increasing task diversity increases the richness of a model's internal representations.

</details>


### [245] [Matched-Context Group Policy Optimization for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13755)
*Chenxu Zhang,Yaorui Shi,Wenlong Zhang,Jianchao Tan,Zihan Wang,Yiming Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Training long-horizon language model agents requires assigning credit to intermediate decisions from sparse terminal feedback. Within a finite group of sibling rollouts, useful action comparisons depend on their context, evidence for both alternatives, and how that evidence enters learning. We propose Matched-Context Group Policy Optimization (MCGPO), a procedure for constructing weighted action preferences from already collected trajectories. MCGPO defines comparison contexts with pre-action state summaries and observed rubric progress, annotated by task rules. Two-action support selects among ordered exact and coarse matching rules. Fixed folds separate candidate decisions from the terminal outcomes used to orient their preferences, and support-dependent evidence weights scale an auxiliary pairwise objective alongside shared GraphGPO task learning. MCGPO reuses rollout data and each response's original history, preserving critic-free training. Across six environment-backbone settings with three training seeds each, MCGPO improves mean success over matched GraphGPO: by 0.47-1.46 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-1.5B and 7B, and by 3.96 and 5.16 points on WebShop with Qwen3-4B and 8B. The ALFWorld gains build on baseline success above 92%, where remaining headroom is limited. Integrated success improves in three of four Qwen2.5 settings. Three-seed factorial controls support opposite-fold evidence and evidence weighting; joint state-rubric matching achieves the highest mean in all four representation-control settings.

</details>


### [246] [When Edge Independence Fails: Joint Graph Diffusion with Latent Sociability Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13765)
*Adarsh Jamadandi,Nicolas Keriven,Aline Roumy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Discrete graph diffusion models corrupt graph structure through independent edge updates with a Markovian noise model. Combined with the exchangeability requirement on the output distribution, the terminal prior is necessarily an Erdős--Rényi (E-R) random graph. This has a fundamental consequence for unconditional graph generation without node features: in the large graph limit, any GNN denoiser applied to an E-R graph approximately outputs an E-R graph, making effective denoising difficult. In practice, generated graphs fail to recover meaningful node heterogeneity and higher-order structure. Previous models introduced specialized node features to compensate, framing this as an expressivity problem, while the role of the prior remained unexamined. We instead address the root cause by introducing the Latent Sociability Prior (LSP) and a join} diffusion process that co-evolves latent node sociability variables and graph structure, removing edge independence while preserving exchangeability. Unlike existing latent graph diffusion methods, the sociability mechanism requires no complex encoder: its sole role is to guide edge updates and preserve node heterogeneity throughout the trajectory. We validate our approach on multiple graph generation datasets and show that the proposed framework more accurately captures graph distributions than baseline methods without node features.

</details>


### [247] [DualQuant: Dual-scale Calibration-free Weight-and-Activation Quantization for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13767)
*Mahsa Salmani,Sayeh Sharify,Hesham Mostafa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Low-bit inference on accelerators with native 4-bit support requires quantizing both weights and activations to realize the full throughput gains these formats provide. While post-training weight quantization has been extensively studied, activation quantization remains considerably more challenging and typically requires calibration data. In this work, we introduce DUALQUANT, a dual-scale, calibration-free post-training quantization framework that directly minimizes the weight-reconstruction Mean Squared Error (MSE) and, as an immediate by-product, yields per-channel activation rescalings prior to activation quantization. Our key observation is that the scaling factors obtained by minimizing weight-reconstruction error provide effective activation rescalings, enabling competitive activation quantization without calibration data. We formulate DUALQUANT as a non-differentiable weight-reconstruction problem and develop an efficient solver based on variable splitting and block coordinate descent, where each subproblem admits a closed-form update. DUALQUANT provides a unified algorithm supporting a wide range of formats, including the full MX family and NVFP4. To our knowledge, DUALQUANT is the first dual-scaling method to formally support MX and NV block formats. Across Llama and Qwen models evaluated on WikiText-2, C4, and zero-shot downstream tasks, DUALQUANT matches or exceeds the accuracy of calibration-based SmoothQuant across most models and formats, and substantially outperforms calibration-free baselines, with large gains for MX and NV block formats. We validate these gains with a real packed W4A4 inference path for Llama-3.1-8B on an RTX 4090. The resulting implementation closely matches the accuracy of fake quantization while delivering up to 2.61x memory reduction and up to 2.46x throughput speedup over BF16.

</details>


### [248] [ReplaySSM: Cache SSM Inputs, Not State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13778)
*Ze-Wei Liou,Harris Nover,Amey Naik,Andrii Skliar,Igor Shovkun,Ido Shahaf,Tri Dao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: State-space models (SSMs) maintain a fixed-size recurrent state, but reading and writing it at every decoding step can bottleneck hybrid models. This eager state management causes large state I/O with low arithmetic intensity, leaves speculative decoding no cheap rollback, and feeds state quantization error into subsequent recurrent updates. We introduce ReplaySSM, which unrolls the recurrence to represent the state as a checkpoint and a buffer of recent SSM inputs. This separates output computation from checkpoint updates. By updating the checkpoint periodically, ReplaySSM avoids state writes on most steps and roughly halves the dominant state memory traffic. Keeping recent inputs also makes speculative rollback a buffer-pointer operation and enables output-only verification without materializing intermediate states. The same separation allows a low-precision checkpoint to serve output computation on most steps while a higher-precision checkpoint serves recurrent updates, so rounding error in the low-precision copy never enters later updates. We implement ReplaySSM in vLLM for Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet. At serving batch size 256, it accelerates standard decoding by $1.20$--$1.47\times$ over a tuned vLLM baseline across four hybrid models, and delivers about $1.8\times$ the throughput of vLLM's speculative decoding on the two 120B-class models. Separate state precisions also reduce output KL divergence and downstream accuracy losses compared with a shared low-precision checkpoint.

</details>


### [249] [Your Discrete Graph Diffusion Model is Secretly Memorizing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13786)
*Adarsh Jamadandi,Nicolas Keriven*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Discrete graph diffusion models (GDMs) corrupt the graph structure by adding noise independently to nodes and edges under a Markovian noise model. In the unconditional setting without node features, an equivariant denoiser trained on a single graph is able to generate novel graphs and sample-level metrics such as edge overlap and WL kernel similarity find no evidence of memorization. In this work, we challenge this view and show for the first time that graph diffusion models indeed exhibit memorization. Analyzing the reverse diffusion trajectories of GDMs trained on two-block stochastic block models (SBMs), we find a strong train-data bias: the model recovers a corrupted training graph almost perfectly compared to a held-out test graph. We term this conditional memorization, as it surfaces only when the reverse trajectory is conditioned on a corrupted training graph and is not apparent during inference. It coincides with a growing generalization gap, where the training loss improves as the loss on held-out graphs degrades, and it cannot be mitigated by early stopping. The onset of memorization always precedes the emergence of community structure in the generated graphs, thus every checkpoint earlier produces structureless graphs. This behavior holds across all node sizes we test.

</details>


### [250] [Randomized Momentum Transport with Cheap Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13789)
*El Mahdi Chayti,Martin Jaggi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Momentum methods such as STORM and second-order momentum track the gradient by adding a transport correction to momentum, at the cost of an extra oracle call per iteration. We introduce randomized momentum transport, which evaluates the correction with probability $q$ and otherwise uses a cheap prediction, with importance weighting that preserves the conditional mean and adds $(q^{-1}-1)$ times the prediction loss to the variance. Under transport-bias, second-moment, and residual-control assumptions, linear minimization oracle (LMO) updates, including normalized, sign, and exact spectral (Muon) directions, retain an $O(T^{-1/3})$ stationarity bound for each fixed $q>0$ with a single-gradient initialization. For unnormalized STORM, some $q<1$ lowers the leading coefficient of our sufficient gradient budget if and only if the gradient-difference noise modulus exceeds the smoothness constant, by at most about $10\%$. An online diagonal predictor admits a regret-to-convergence guarantee. At approximately equal oracle cost, querying a quarter of the corrections lowers the final training loss by $18$ to $43\%$ on MNIST1D and raises the test accuracy of Hessian-vector transport on CIFAR-10 by $1.5$ points.

</details>


### [251] [FORTE: A Production Framework for Feature Evaluation and Deprecation in Industrial Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13806)
*Vishnu Arun,Adam Obeng,Tianyou Wang,Zilin Ren,Mukuntha Narayanan Sundararaman,Han Wang,Shivin Thukral,Zhixiang Wang,Saurabh Vishwas Joshi,Darren Reger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Production recommender systems have feature-lifecycle asymmetry: adding new features must produce gains on some metric, while feature removal must not regress any metric. In practice, feature deprecation is hard to prioritize as a feature can fail to be removed if it interacts with other features, its importance changes daily, it cannot be evaluated before production models change or data drift occurs, it is in contention for A/B test space with other launches, it causes regressions in an online A/B test, or it is consumed by multiple models that need to coordinate its deprecation. We present FORTE, a model-agnostic production framework for discovering, validating, and removing features. Its efficient perturbation implementation allows daily feature evaluation. To account for interactions, it evaluates candidate feature sets constructed from all the low-rank features through grouped ablation and its offline results are sufficiently predictive of online A/B tests to allow deprecations to proceed to online testing with confidence. Lineage tracing identifies all downstream consumer models to realize the full savings of removing a feature from the training set. We validate FORTE over 2 large-scale recommender systems and find that it identifies deprecable feature sets which do not cause online regressions, while the impact on efficiency depends on model characteristics. On a search-relevance model, FORTE resulted in 3 nested candidate sets of 32, 48, and 59 features. Online guardrails rejected the largest treatment. The 48-feature set was selected for deprecation, removing 43.3% of the model's features. On a large ranking model, FORTE identified 8 candidates for removal. Retraining and A/B testing confirmed no regression. Its launch reduced the trainable parameters by 29.9%, improved serving throughput by 19.8-25.5%, and reduced annual training costs by hundreds of thousands of dollars.

</details>


### [252] [QTestBench: A Test Framework for Evaluating LLM-Generated Quantum Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13811)
*Meriem Terki,Nouhaila Innan,Minghao Shao,Muhammad Kashif,Alberto Marchisio,Muhammad Shafique*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong results in classical code generation and are increasingly used to write the unit tests that safeguard that code. Whether they can do the same for quantum software is less clear, because quantum programs are probabilistic, depend on numerical tolerances, and are judged by domain-specific checkers, so a well-formed test can still disagree with the benchmark's verdict. Existing quantum benchmarks measure whether a model produces a correct solution, but not whether its tests can distinguish correct implementations from faulty ones. We introduce QTestBench, a framework that evaluates LLM-generated quantum test suites against independent benchmark checkers, never treating the tests themselves as ground truth. For each task, QTestBench prompts models to produce syntactic, semantic, and behavioral tests, injects candidate solutions at runtime, and compares each test verdict with the checker's label under three validation cohorts, each answering a distinct diagnostic question. We benchmark five LLMs on eight PennyLane QHack challenges and ten Qiskit HumanEval tasks. Generated suites almost always execute, but discrimination lags behind: the best generator reaches a test quality of 0.71 on QHack and 0.34 on Qiskit HumanEval, so executability alone overstates test quality. Oracle-verified synthetic mutants stabilize bug-detection estimates when natural faulty solutions are scarce, as on three of ten Qiskit tasks that yield none. QTestBench offers a reproducible, cross-framework benchmark for the trustworthiness of LLM-generated quantum tests.

</details>


### [253] [Spanning Diffusion and Flow-based Posterior Sampling via Exact Gaussian Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13812)
*Teo Deveney,Lea Bogensperger,Hok Shing Wong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Flow-matching and diffusion models are increasingly used as priors for linear inverse problems, however, most methods are bespoke to one class of trajectory prior. Plug-and-play methods have been proposed, however these implicitly decorrelate the guided trajectory, resulting in broad priors that can struggle when data is sparse. This work proposes $β$-Flow: a general plug-and-play method indexed by a correlation parameter $β$ that generalises over flow, diffusion, and decorrelated priors, and a confidence parameter $κ$ that controls how strongly the path distribution is enforced. Each step requires only one forward evaluation of a denoising network, followed by a Gaussian conditioning step. This step is an exact Kalman update that is not dependent on network gradients. We show that many existing guided samplers can be interpreted as instances or approximations of our method, each implicitly using a different trajectory prior and operator in place of the Kalman gain. From this viewpoint, we provide a qualitative comparison of methods in terms of their implicit biases and assumptions. We demonstrate consistently state-of-the-art level performance across 7 inverse problems over 4 datasets in terms of PSNR and SSIM. Moreover, we demonstrate the utility of this prior control, by showing that confident high correlations (strict priors) work best when observations are less informative, while low correlation is preferred when observations are more informative, reflecting the complementary roles of the prior and likelihood in Bayesian inference.

</details>


### [254] [TabMF: Learning Dataset Representations with Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13818)
*Rafiq Kamel,Nicolas Atienza,Aziz Fouché,Nicola Cancedda,Josif Grabocka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Representations of supervised datasets support higher-level machine-learning tasks such as dataset retrieval and distribution-shift monitoring. However, existing approaches typically rely either on hand-crafted meta-features or on dataset encoders trained specifically for representation learning. We introduce TabMF, a framework that repurposes pretrained tabular foundation models (TFMs) as encoders of supervised datasets. TabMF equips a predictive TFM with an architecture-aware dataset-level readout and adapts the model end-to-end using contrastive post-training on stochastic views of synthetic supervised datasets. We instantiate TabMF on TabICLv2 and TabPFN-2.5 and evaluate the resulting representations across six tasks on unseen real datasets. Simple frozen TFM readouts yield competitive representations, indicating that predictive pretraining already induces substantial dataset-level structure. The two TabMF encoders obtain the strongest aggregate performance among the evaluated learned and classical dataset representations, with particularly strong results in dataset retrieval, compact benchmark construction, and distribution-shift severity assessment.

</details>


### [255] [Shared Canonical Component Removal Beyond Expert Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13819)
*Jose Roberto Tello Ayala,Akl C. Fahed,Roy Perlis,Finale Doshi-Velez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: In clinical practice, experts often begin by converting raw inputs into a set of expert features before making a decision. While black-box models trained on raw input can outperform models trained on expert views, it remains unclear what predictive signal the raw model exploits beyond what the expert view captures. We introduce an audit framework to quantify this gap using Shared Canonical Component Removal (SCCR). Unlike standard residualization or concept erasure, SCCR selectively removes only those directions strongly shared between learned raw and expert embeddings.
  Evaluations on synthetic data, coronary artery disease (CAD) detection, antidepressant treatment dropout prediction, and skin lesion classification using SkinCon annotations show that SCCR identifies the ways in which expert views are insufficient and recovers residual predictive signal while minimizing redundancy with the expert view.

</details>


### [256] [On the Design Space of Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13834)
*Oscar Davis,Daan Roos,Alice Bizeul,Amitis Shidani,Victor Turrisi,Louis Béthune,Pierre Ablin,Marco Cuturi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Continuous diffusion language models (CDLMs) promise to bring the benefits of continuous flow modelling to language, including accelerated sampling and controllable generation. The field has grown rapidly, and the capabilities of CDLMs have begun to rival those of discrete diffusion. Yet, unlike for continuous modalities such as images or videos, our grasp of the levers that matter when training CDLMs remains limited. In this work, we focus on two of these levers, namely self-conditioning and embedding geometry, and shed light on some of the mechanisms behind them, which in turn suggest cheaper and more effective designs. We begin by showing that self-conditioning corrects a train/inference mismatch by exposing the model to inference-like states that are otherwise rare during training. These gains can be recovered without an extra forward pass by a two-level interpolant, coined $\mathrm{DoubleCat}$, which improves TinyGSM accuracy more than threefold. Self-conditioning also aids in recognising tokens dominated by large-norm Gaussians$\unicode{x2013}$an issue known as token identifiability. To address it directly, we introduce discrete reveals to $\mathrm{DoubleCat}$, yielding $\mathrm{SemiCat}$, which achieves discrete diffusion-level performance on TinyGSM, reaching $49.94\%$ with standard training hyperparameters and a tuned sampler. At the $1.7$B scale, $\mathrm{SemiCat}$ outperforms discrete diffusion on four of six likelihood-based evaluations, and surpasses one-hot flows on unconditional generation. On embedded spaces, we find that cross-entropy drives embeddings towards a geometry whose decoding error rate closely matches the one-hot one. We briefly explore a range of further options, such as hybrid corruption processes and noise schedules, including asynchronous ones. We believe this exploration can help the community better understand and advance the design space of CDLMs.

</details>


### [257] [Specialists Learn Faster: Knowledge Organization in Pre-training Shapes Knowledge Update Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13835)
*Sheng Zhao,Yuhao Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: An ideal (language) model must continually update its knowledge. Existing work largely focuses on making these updates efficient after training. We investigate how the organization of time-varying knowledge during pre-training shapes the efficiency of subsequent updates. Models trained on chronologically ordered knowledge specialize in the most recently encountered knowledge; we call them specialists. In contrast, models trained on the same knowledge in randomly shuffled order perform well across all represented time periods; we call them generalists. Surprisingly, specialists require far fewer update steps than generalists to acquire new knowledge. To explain this advantage, we derive a law from first principles that relates update cost to the model's optimization dynamics and that closely predicts empirical results. Guided by these findings, we design a strategy for organizing pre-training data that preserves stable historical knowledge while enabling efficient updates to current knowledge. We also show that the benefits of temporal knowledge organization extend to real-world pre-trained models and existing knowledge update tools, and generalize to other knowledge orderings. Project page: https://horizon-lab.org/specialist/

</details>


### [258] [Returning to Development States: When Full Sandbox Snapshots Help Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13841)
*Daniel Thi Graviet,Tanmay Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Coding agents often make useful progress before they fail, but retry-from-scratch discards the machine state created along the way. This makes failure recovery an interesting search problem: if intermediate environment states preserve meaningful progress, restoring and branching from them could outperform repeatedly restarting from the initial state. We implement Go-Explore-style search over full Daytona snapshots and find that higher and more efficient solve rates depend mainly on progress that sits outside the repository, such as databases, services, and other runtime state. Our results suggest that full-snapshot search is most effective when long-horizon coding work spans multiple services whose state cannot be reconstructed from a single diff.

</details>


### [259] [Inference-Time Steering Across Scaffolds and Epitopes for Sample-Efficient De Novo Nanobody Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13844)
*Mason Minot,Simon Friedensohn,Cédric R. Weber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Generative molecular design typically relies on over-generation and post-hoc filtering. Inference-time steering methods like Feynman-Kac (FK) steering improve efficiency by biasing trajectories toward high-reward candidates, yet they optimize within a single fixed configuration. In de novo nanobody design, upfront commitment to conditioning choices like scaffold identity or target epitope creates a bottleneck. Since yield varies across these conditions, steering unpromising setups wastes compute. We recast generative condition selection as an inference-time resource-allocation problem. We propose two dynamic budget allocators: a joint Global Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) formulation that steers and allocates compute across configurations concurrently, and a decoupled approach adapting Batched Successive Elimination (BaSE). Empirically, these dynamic allocators increase the yield of designable candidates per GPU-hour nearly 5$\times$ without sacrificing diversity across distinct therapeutic targets and generative configurations.

</details>


### [260] [Partially Informed Stochastic Optimization via Zeroth-Order Residual Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13871)
*El Mahdi Chayti,Alireza Mirrokni,Martin Jaggi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Many optimization problems provide direct access to only part of the gradient, while the remaining component must be inferred from function evaluations. This structure arises in performative prediction, performative reinforcement learning, and bilevel optimization with a black-box lower level. We introduce Partially Informed Stochastic Optimization (PISO), which combines the available gradient component with a running estimate of the residual. By centring each zeroth-order observation on this estimate, PISO makes the variance due to random probing depend on the tracking error rather than the residual's magnitude. This yields a randomized sketch iteration with an explicit contraction mechanism and a principled interpretation of momentum. For smooth nonconvex objectives, we establish convergence guarantees that depend on the residual's variation and initial magnitude, without requiring a uniform bound on the unavailable gradient component. With independently sampled function evaluations and Gaussian or spherical probes, PISO attains leading function-evaluation complexities of \(O(d^{2}\varepsilon^{-6})\) under Lipschitz gradients and \(O(d^{2}\varepsilon^{-5})\) under an additional Lipschitz-Hessian assumption. These rates require only one probe direction, two function samples, and one known-component sample per update. The analysis also quantifies how batching trades observations for fewer deployments. Across five settings, PISO variants improve on the tested baselines with comparable oracle access in three, reach a comparable solution faster in bilevel routing, and perform best among methods without response-model derivatives in performative reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [261] [Prediction Is Not a Sufficient Memory State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13881)
*Robert Jenkinson Alvarez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: A memory that reproduces every current prediction may still have forgotten how to learn from the next observation. We characterize this distinction for exact cumulative learning and attention. For smooth convex residual losses with unrestricted real features and labels, a continuous recurrent state of fixed dimension exists exactly when the loss is polynomial. With ordinary, unweighted observations, degree $D$ requires precisely $r=\binom{d+D}{D}-1$ coordinates; even among histories with one fixed current predictor, exactly $r-d$ coordinates can remain necessary for continuation. Nonpolynomial losses have unbounded requirements on such equal-prediction histories. Bounded scalar logistic examples retain this obstruction after fixing any finite derivative jet. For ridge regression, the hidden evidence is the design matrix: the exact state costs $dp+d(d+1)/2$, versus $dp$ for the predictor. On a well-conditioned zero-prediction family, optimal $B$-bit compression has average one-update error of order $d^{-1}2^{-2B/s_d}$, $s_d=d(d+1)/2$, even with arbitrary randomized codes. Kernel attention gives a second exact closure classification. The results identify what future updates reveal, rather than proposing a new regression algorithm or asserting trained-model gains.

</details>


### [262] [Revisiting Tabular Deep Regression: Objectives and the Limits of Global Early Stopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13909)
*Danil Rudenko,Georgii Bulgakov,Nikolay Kartashev,Ivan Rubachev,Daniil Krasylnikov,Artem Babenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: While deep learning models for tabular data have recently received significant research attention, the choice of objective functions for regression tasks remains an understudied degree of freedom. Several lines of research offer alternatives to the standard Mean Squared Error (MSE), but differences in datasets, backbones, and evaluation protocols make their practical benefits difficult to compare. In this work, we address this gap by performing a systematic re-evaluation of regression objectives across a broad collection of established tabular datasets and multiple neural backbones. Our comparison reveals that many proposed objectives provide limited or inconsistent gains over a properly tuned MSE baseline, while quantile loss yields consistent aggregate improvements over MSE across the tested backbones. We then identify a limitation that remains even with this strong objective: regions with different levels of target noise can reach their best predictive performance at different training epochs. Consequently, a single global early-stopping checkpoint can impose a compromise between uncertainty regions, even when the objective can represent heteroskedastic target distributions. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce a heteroskedasticity-aware extension of quantile loss that uses input-dependent label smoothing as a potential remedy and improves over quantile loss on several backbones. Overall, our results provide a modern empirical picture of regression objectives for tabular deep learning and highlight how differences in optimal stopping times across uncertainty regions can limit the effectiveness of global early stopping. The code is available at https://github.com/yandex-research/tabular-regression.

</details>


### [263] [Designing Protein Structures from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13917)
*Joseph Lee,Yanjing Li,Bo Qiang,Gregory Lau,Allyson Ettinger,Noah A. Smith,Rory Donovan-Maiye,David Baker,Hanna Hajishirzi,Pang Wei Koh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: A central challenge in de novo protein design is generating proteins with desired structural and functional properties. Existing design models typically require a dedicated input representation for each property, limiting the design objectives they support. We develop Prompteina, the first all-atom structure generation model that supports new and broader design objectives specified in natural language, analogous to text-to-image generation. To train Prompteina, we construct Prompteina-Data, a paired text--protein structure dataset of 1.8M structures and 50M+ captions describing global and residue-specific structural and functional properties. We adapt Proteina-Complexa, a pretrained structure generation model, through text-conditioned pretraining and finetuning that aligns residues with their corresponding text. Prompteina outperforms baselines on 15 of 18 structural properties and generates proteins that more closely match descriptions of their intended functions. In binder design, it leads baselines on at least 13 of 20 targets and improves interface confidence by 14\% over the strongest baseline. With iterative in silico feedback, Prompteina's gain over non-iterative generation is 57\% larger than the next-best baseline on the hardest targets. Finally, we experimentally validate Prompteina designs against GM2A, a target with no reported de novo binders. We identify nine binders, with the strongest at an apparent $K_{\mathrm{D}}$ of 107 nM. These results establish natural language as an effective and powerful conditioning interface for protein structure generation.

</details>


### [264] [KL Before and After Mixing: A Round-Complexity Separation in Parallel Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13924)
*Tianyi Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: A parallel sampler can choose at random which coordinates to generate together. For a target $p$ and the output law $q_U$ of a schedule $U$ drawn afresh for each output, we compare the schedule-averaged divergence $\mathbb{E}_U D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|q_U)$ with the output-law divergence $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|\mathbb{E}_U q_U)$, in nats. Rounds commit independent draws from exact conditional marginals frozen at the round's start, each output's schedule is drawn before sampling from a finite mixture of schedules, and every scheduled round is charged. For the uniform distribution on binary strings of even length $n$ with $n/2$ ones, the least expected number of rounds at a fixed tolerance $\varepsilon>0$ is $Θ_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ under the first criterion but $(e^{-\varepsilon}+o(1))\log_2\ln n$ under the second, and the double-logarithmic order is attainable with the same round count on every run. For this target, a count-state recursion gives the exact least output divergence at every round cap over all causal randomized policies. The separation extends to weighted fixed-count targets with bounded weight ratios and quota fractions bounded away from zero and one, without permutation symmetry, and to the full-support Beta$(1,1)$--Bernoulli target, where the sharp expected cost under the output-law criterion is $(e^{-\varepsilon}/2+o(1))\log_2\log_2n$, attained with rare $n$-round runs. Prior work implies the order of the schedule-averaged cost on the balanced target and its $O_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ upper bounds on both extensions.

</details>


### [265] [AI Models Can Provably Hide Arbitrary Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13928)
*Andis Draguns,Sumeet Ramesh Motwani,Raymond Douglas,Christian Schroeder de Witt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 

Method: 

Result: 

Conclusion: 

Abstract: Capability evaluations aim to surface dangerous model behaviors before deployment, but their reliability depends on the assumption that hidden capabilities can be elicited. We show this assumption does not hold under adversarial conditions by constructing backdoor attacks that embed encrypted neural networks within host model weights, decrypting and executing them only upon receiving a secret trigger. Using digital lockers, these hidden circuits remain provably hard to elicit and interpret under cryptographic assumptions, even given full access to model weights, extending prior work from hiding fixed strings to arbitrary computations. We implement our proof of concept constructions in PyTorch and evaluate elicitation through fine-tuning, weight noising, gradient-based trigger search, and latent adversarial training on a small chemical reaction prediction task - a proxy for CBRN-relevant capabilities. Our results demonstrate resistance to these methods and, together with our cryptographic guarantees, reveal a limit of elicitation-based capability audits. We release our models to support the improvement of defenses.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)
